Inferring Ambient Cycles of Point Samples on Manifolds with Universal Coverings
Ka Man Yim

Abstract
A central objective of topological data analysis is to identify topologically significant features in data represented as a finite point cloud. We consider the setting where the ambient space of the point sample is a compact Riemannian manifold. Given a simplicial complex constructed on the point set, we can relate the first homology of the complex with that of the ambient manifold by matching edges in the complex with minimising geodesics between points. Provided the universal covering of the manifold is known, we give a constructive method for identifying whether a given edge loop (or representative first homology cycle) on the complex corresponds to a non-trivial loop (or first homology class) of the ambient manifold. We show that metric data on the point cloud and its fibre in the covering suffices for the construction, and formalise our approach in the framework of groupoids and monodromy of coverings.



1
Introduction

A recurring theme in topological data analysis is to analyse the ‘shape’ of a subset
A\subseteq X
in relation to the geometry and topology of the ambient space. Out of the many variations on tackling this problem, we focus on analysing whether
X
inherits the non-trivial loops and cycles of the ambient space. For example, we wish to infer the induced homomomorphism on the first homology group by the inclusion of
A
into
M
. If a cycle in
A
maps onto a non-trivial cycle in
X
, we call those the
ambient cycles
of
A
in
X
.

Here, we restrict ourselves to the setting where
M
is a compact Riemannian manifold, and, the shape in question we want to analyse is based on a finite point cloud in
M
. For example, this shape can be the
\epsilon
-thickening of the point cloud. We consider this task in the setting where the manifold is equipped with its
universal covering
. The universal covering is often accessible for model manifolds, such as tori, projective spaces, and compact surfaces with uniform curvature. We show how data inferred from the universal covering of the ambient manifold allows us to infer a covering of the simplicial complex, induced by an embedding of the complex. We show how such data can be integrated to infer the homomorphism on the fundamental group and first homology group induced by the embedding. We first set out the theoretical framework of this approach, which is based on a groupoids description of covering spaces. After setting out the abstract framework, we describe the practical pipeline for processing point clouds on such manifolds, where metric information of the manifolds are leveraged to make the inference.

Coverings and Groupoids

The theory of covering spaces relates the fundamental group of a space
X
with a symmetry on another space
\tilde{X}
. An exposition of the theory of coverings is given in
Section

3
. We summarise the key objects of interest in this introduction. In a class of coverings called
regular coverings
(or
\Gamma
-coverings), the base space
X
is the orbit space of a
\Gamma
-action on
\tilde{X}
, and the quotient
p:\tilde{X}\twoheadrightarrow X
induces a short exact sequence of fundamental groups

The homomorphism
\mu
, which we call the
monodromy homomorphism
, relates loops in
X
with the
\Gamma
-action on
p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)}
via the homotopy lifting property of coverings. Monodromy classifies
\Gamma
-coverings up to isomorphism. The exactness of the sequence implies loops in
\pi_{1}(X)
can be categorised into those that descend from the covering space
\tilde{X}
(i.e. in the image of
\pi_{1}(p)
), and those that are generated due to the quotient by the group action (i.e. with non-trivial monodromy). In the case where
\tilde{X}
is simply connected, the covering is the
universal covering
of the base space
X
. Since the sequence above is exactness, the monodromy
\mu
is an isomorphism between
{\pi_{1}}({X})
and
\Gamma
. If we have a continuous map
f:X\to Y
, the universal covering of
q:\tilde{Y}\twoheadrightarrow Y
induces a
\Gamma
-covering
p:\tilde{X}\twoheadrightarrow X
, where
G\cong\pi_{1}(Y)
. The monodromy
\mu_{X}:{\pi_{1}}({X})\to\Gamma
of the induced covering on
X
in fact recovers the induced homomorphism
{\pi_{1}}({f}):{\pi_{1}}({X})\to{\pi_{1}}({Y})
up to isomorphism.

From the point of view of our application to simplicial complexes embedded in an ambient manifold, if we know the universal covering of the ambient manifold, then a recovery of the induced covering on the complex suffices to infer
{\pi_{1}}({f})
. In our setting, the induced covering of the complex is encoded as a
transition homomorphism
(here viewing
p
as a principal
\Gamma
-bundle). We thus focus on how monodromy of a covering can be inferred from its transition homomorphism. Given a good cover
\mathcal{U}
of
X
, a transition homomorphism prescribes how a
\Gamma
-covering
p:\tilde{X}\twoheadrightarrow X
can be reconstructed by gluing together fibres
p^{-1}{\left({U}\right)}
of
U\in\mathcal{U}
; this consists of associating elements
g_{ij}\in\Gamma
to pairs of cover elements
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
with non-empty intersection. In other words, it is an attachment of elements of
\Gamma
to edges in the
nerve

\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}
of the open cover. If the open cover is finite (which can be made so whenever
X
is compact), then the transition homomorphism is a finite description of the
\Gamma
-covering. We employ the formalism of
groupoids
1
1

1

We recall a groupoid is a (small) category where all morphisms are invertible, and a groupoid homomorphism is simply a functor between groupoids. See
Section

2
.
to relate the transition homomorphism with monodromy. In more precise terms, the transition homomorphism is a groupoid homomorphism
t:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\to\Gamma
on the
edge groupoid

\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
of the nerve. In the way which the nerve of a good cover
\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}
is a discrete combinatorial topological representation of
X
, the edge groupoid is a discrete representation of the
fundamental groupoid
2
2

2

While the fundamental group describes the algebra of homotopy classes of loops based at a single point, the fundamental groupoid describes the algebra of homotopy classes of paths between any pair of points in
X
.

\Pi{{X}}
of the space. There is in fact an analogue of homotopy equivalence that relates the two groupoids. We show in
Section

3.2
how monodromy and the transition homomorphism encode equivalent data. This equivalence is summarised by the following commutative diagram:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{37.02063pt}{-5.74606pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Here the monodromy homomorphism
\mu
here is promoted from a group homomorphism on the fundamental group to a groupoid homomorphism on the fundamental groupoid. This equivalence underpins the practical applications or our methods, as it allows us to summarise monodromy on compact spaces using finite data expressed in the transition homomorphism
t
on the nerve of the covering
\mathcal{U}
. Returning to the context of simplicial complexes embedded in an ambient space
f:|K|\to X
, we consider the transition homomorphism
t
of the induced covering on
|K|
by
f
; because the monodromy
\mu
of the induced covering recovers the induced homomorphism of the fundamental groups
{\pi_{1}}({f}):{\pi_{1}}({|K|})\to{\pi_{1}}({X})
, we can deduce
{\pi_{1}}({f})
by constructing the transition homomorphism
t
of the induced covering.

We also discuss the homology of groupoids. In particular, taking the first homology of the fundamental groupoid recovers the first singular homology of the underlying space. Likewise, the first homology of an edge groupoid of a simplicial complex is isomorphic to its first simplicial homology. Moreover, these relationships are natural isomorphisms. This allows us to translate groupoid homomorphisms between fundamental groupoids, edge groupoids, and groups, to homomorphisms between homology. This is especially important as we translate data from the transition homomorphisms
t
of induced coverings to recover homomorphisms
{H_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({X}\right)\mathclose{}\to{H_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({Y}\right)\mathclose{}
.

Applications to Topological Data Analysis on Manifolds

We focus on the geometric setting where we have a continuous map
f:X\to M
, where
M
is a complete Riemannian manifold, and
p
is the universal Riemannian covering — that is,
p
is also a local isometry.

Like most methods in standard topological data analysis, we assume the input data is a finite set of points
\X=\left\{{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}}\right\}
in
M
. We first consider
X=\X^{\epsilon}
, the
\epsilon
-thickening of
\X
, and
f
being the inclusion of
\X^{\epsilon}
into
M
. Here we require
\epsilon
to be less than the geodesic convexity radius of
M
to facilitate a good cover of
\X^{\epsilon}
. We show how we can infer the transition homomorphism
t
of the induced covering on
X
, and thus infer the induced homomorphism of
f
on the fundamental group or first homology. The required information to infer
t
consists simply of the distances between points in
\X
, and the distances between points in their fibres
p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)}\subset\tilde{M}
.

In a sparse sampling case where distances between points may exceed the geodesic convexity radius, we consider another approach to simulating the shape of the point cloud rather than considering its
\epsilon
-thickening. Here we consider a graph
G
on the vertex set
\X
, and consider a map
f:|G|\to M
that sends edges to minimising geodesics on
M
. For example,
G
can be an
\epsilon
-neighbourhood graph or a
k
-nn graph on
\X
. We show that minimising geodesics can be uniquely assigned to pairs of points in
\X
for generic configurations of
\X
. In this construction, the same metric data in the case for
\X^{\epsilon}
suffices to infer the transition homomorphism
t:\mathcal{E}{{G}}\to\Gamma
. Using
t
, we follow the same recipe to find the induced homomorphisms
{\pi_{1}}({f})
and
{H_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({f}\right)\mathclose{}
.

Finally, as an application, we use this computational framework to empirically analyse a geometric signature of measures on manifolds called
principal persistence measures

[
11
]
. The one-dimensional principal persistence measure is a geometric summary of the distribution of four point configurations sampled from the measure. In particular, it encodes the geometry of cycles that can be formed on four point configurations by minimising geodesic interpolations. We show how we can decompose the support of the principal persistence measure of an empirical sample on a manifold by the homology class of cycles to help interpret the geometric and topological features of the measure detected by its principal persistence measure.

Related Work

Within topological data analysis, the relationship between covering spaces, monodromy, and the homology of subcomplexes in such spaces was first explored in
[
21
]
. The focus of that study was on detecting loops and one-cycles of complexes in
\Z^{n}
periodic spaces that are generated by the periodic symmetry. In
[
22
]
the authors extend their work to higher homology groups for complexes in
\Z
-periodic spaces. Here we only consider one-dimensional homology, but our approach generalises to complexes in universal covering spaces with group action
\Gamma
not necessarily
\Z^{n}
.

2
Groupoids

In this section, we discuss the algebraic and categorical framework that describes homotopy classes of paths on a topological space
X
– the
fundamental groupoid

\Pi{{X}}
. The fundamental groupoid generalises the fundamental group to describe homotopy classes of paths between any two fixed end points, in addition to loops.

By framing our problem in terms of groupoids, we reap some conceptual benefits. First, the groupoid formalism provides a natural framework to relate paths to simplicial approximations of paths. If a space
X
admits a good cover
\mathcal{U}
, then we can relate the fundamental groupoid to the
edge groupoid

\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
of the nerve of the cover via a pair of groupoid homomorphism
R:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\leftrightarrows\Pi{{X}}:S
. In a similar fashion to the fundamental groupoid, the edge groupoid describes equivalence classes of edge paths on a simplicial complex, related by a combinatorial notion of homotopy. In
Proposition

2.12
we describe how the pair of groupoid homomorphism
R
and
S
describe an equivalence (of categories) between the fundamental and edge groupoids. Regarding
\mathcal{E}
and
\Pi
as functors from simplicial complexes to groupoids, we also show that
R
is a natural isomorphism between those two functors in
Proposition

2.14
.

In
Section

2.3
we review basic facts about the homology of groupoids, and how it relates to the homology of spaces.
Groupoid homology

\mathcal{H}_{\ast}:\Grpd\to\AbGrp
is a functor from the category of groupoids to abelian groups. In particular, the groupoid homology of a group is equivalent to its group homology. Furthermore, we recall the first group homology is the abelianisation of a group
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({\Gamma}\right)\mathclose{}\cong{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{ab}}
. Focussing on the fundamental groupoid
\Pi{{X}}
, we consider
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({\Pi{{X}}}\right)\mathclose{}
, which we view as a functor
\TopS\xrightarrow{\Pi}\Grpd\xrightarrow{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\AbGrp
. There is a natural isomorphism
{s}:H_{1}\Rightarrow\mathcal{H}\circ\Pi

Proposition

2.19
. As such, a groupoid homomorphism
\mu:\Pi{{X}}\to\Gamma
induces a homomorphism on singular homology
{s}\circ{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({\mu}\right)\mathclose{}:{H_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({X}\right)\mathclose{}\to{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{ab}}
. We also derive an analogous natural isomorphism between the first homology of edge groupoids and simplicial homology in
Proposition

2.21
.

The benefit of this framework emerges in the subsequent section about covering spaces. We defer detailed discussion on this to
Section

3
, but give a brief account to motivate our study of groupoids. The fundamental groupoid provides a clean framework to describe concepts such as homotopy path-lifting, and coverings induced by continuous maps. In particular, as we focus on
\Gamma
-coverings (coverings generated by quotients of regular group actions), the homotopy lifting of paths are described by a groupoid homomorphism
\mu:\Pi{{X}}\to\Gamma
, which we call the
monodromy homomorphism
. In particular, if the underlying space admits a good cover, we show that
\mu
can be recovered from a discrete summary. Using the homomorphisms
R
and
S
, we can pass between
\mu
and a groupoid homomorphism
t:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\to\Gamma
on the edge groupoid of the nerve of the cover.

We first lay out a few basic definitions and properties of groupoids. We refer to the reader to
[
23
,
4
,
6
]
for a detailed exposition of groupoids and category theory. A
groupoid

\mathsf{C}
is a small category where all morphisms are isomorphisms. For two objects in
a,b\in\mathsf{C}
, we let
\mathsf{C}(a,b)
denote the set of morphisms from
a
to
b
; morphisms
f\in\mathsf{G}(a,b)
and
g\in\mathsf{G}(b,c)
, they compose to form a morphism
fg\in\mathsf{C}(a,c)
; and there is an identity morphism
\iden_{a}\in\mathsf{C}(a,a)
for any
a
. The set of morphisms
\mathsf{C}_{a}:=\mathsf{C}(a,a)
form a group which we call the
vertex group
of
\mathsf{G}
at
a
.

Example 2.1
.

A groupoid with only one object is a group.

Example 2.2
.

A simplicial groupoid
\Delta^{n}
is a groupoid with
n+1
objects; and between any two objects
i
and
j
there is exactly one morphism
\Delta^{n}(i,j)=\{(ij)\}
. This implies
(ij)(jk)=(ik)
for all
i,j,k\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
.

The category of groupoids
\Grpd
is the category where the objects are groupoids and the morphisms are functors
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
between two groupoids as categories. We call morphisms in
\Grpd
groupoid homomorphisms, A
homotopy
of groupoid morphisms
F,G:X\to Y
is a natural isomorphism
\theta:F\Rightarrow G
. Two groupoids are (homotopy)
equivalent
if there is an equivalence of categories
F:\mathsf{C}\rightleftarrows\mathsf{D}:G
. In other words, there are natural isomorphisms
\iden_{\mathsf{C}}\Rightarrow GF
and
FG\Rightarrow\iden_{\mathsf{D}}
. We say
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
defines an
equivalence of groupoids
if there exists a dual
G:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
such that
F
and
G
define an equivalence. As the nomenclature would suggest, an equivalence of categories is an equivalence relation
[
23
, Lemma 1.5.5]
.

Example 2.3
.

If the groupoid
\mathsf{C}
has only one object (i.e. it is a group), then functors
F
and
G
defining an equivalence of categories implies
GF
is an inner automorphism
g\mapsto hgh^{-1}
for some
h\in\mathsf{C}(a,a)
.

Given a groupoid homomorphism
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
, we can determine whether it defines an equivalence without explicitly constructing its dual
G
: a functor
F
is an equivalence of categories iff
F
full and faithful (the map
\mathsf{C}(a,b)\to\mathsf{D}(F(a),F(b))
is a surjective and injective), and essentially surjective (every
d\in\mathsf{D}
is isomorphic to some
F(c)
) (
[
23
, Thm 1.5.9]
). Since the condition of essentially surjective is satisfied by definition on connected groupoids,
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
is an equivalence of groupoids iff
F
is full and faithful.

2.1
The Fundamental Groupoid and the Edge Groupoid

The fundamental groupoid generalises the fundamental group to a groupoid. Rather than considering homotopy classes of loops based on a single base point of a space
X
, the fundamental groupoid
\Pi{{X}}
records the set of homotopy classes of paths between any pair of points in the space. For example, the vertex group
\Pi{{X}}(x,x)
is the fundamental group
{\pi_{1}}({X,x})
. We refer the reader to
[
4
, §6]
for an introductory account to the topic.

Definition 2.4
.

For a topological space
X
, the
fundamental groupoid

\Pi{{X}}
is a groupoid where the objects are the points in
X
, and the set of morphisms
\Pi{{X}}(x,y)
from
x
to
y
are the set of homotopy classes of paths from
x
to
y
in
X
.

Composing morphisms
[\gamma_{1}]\in\Pi{{X}}(x,y)
and
[\gamma_{2}]\in\Pi{{X}}(y,z)
of the fundamental groupoid corresponds to concatenating homotopy classes of paths
[\gamma_{1}]\ast[\gamma_{2}]=[\gamma_{1}\ast\gamma_{2}]\in\Pi{{X}}(x,z)
. The fundamental group
{\pi_{1}}({X,x})=\Pi{{X}}(x,x)
is the group of morphisms from
x
to
x
in the fundamental groupoid; that is, the set of homotopy classes of loops based at
x
.

Indeed, the fundamental groupoid can be regarded as a functor
\Pi:\TopS\to\Grpd
. If
f:X\to Y
is a continuous map, then we have an induced homomorphism of the fundamental groupoids
\Pi{{f}}:\Pi{{X}}\to\Pi{{Y}}
: each homotopy class of paths
[\gamma]\in\Pi{{X}}(x_{0},x_{1})
is sent to a homotopy class of paths
[f\gamma]\in\Pi{{Y}}(f(x_{0}),f(x_{1}))
. A homotopy
F:X\times I\to Y
between maps
f=F(\cdot,0)
and
g=F(\cdot,1)
induces a groupoid homotopy
\Pi_{1}(F):\Pi_{1}(f)\Rightarrow\Pi_{1}(g)
between the induced groupoid morphisms. Naturally, a homotopy equivalence
f:X\rightleftarrows Y:g
induces an equivalence between the fundamental groupoids
\Pi{{f}}:\Pi{{X}}\rightleftarrows Y:\Pi{{g}}

[
4
, 6.5.10]
.

The combinatorial analogue of the fundamental groupoid is the
edge groupoid

\mathcal{E}{{K}}
of a simplicial complex
K
. We introduce the concept following the presentation of
[
6
]
. We first define the corresponding notions of paths and homotopy on
K
.

Definition 2.5
.

An edge path
\eta:I_{m}\to K
on a simplicial complex
K
is a simplicial map from the path graph
I_{m}
with
m
edges to
K
. An edge loop based at
u
is an edge path where
\eta(0)=\eta(m)=u
.

In other words, an edge path is prescribed by a finite sequence of vertices
v_{0}v_{1}\cdots v_{m}
where each successive pair of vertices
v_{i}v_{i+1}
span a simplex in
K
(i.e. either
v_{i}=v_{i+1}
, or
v_{i}v_{i+1}
is an edge). We now describe a combinatorial analogue of homotopy between paths rel end points. Roughly speaking, two edge paths are homotopy equivalent if one can translate one to another over two-simplices of the complex. This is formally described in the following elementary operation between two edge paths.

Definition 2.6
.

An edge path
\eta:I_{m}\to K
is an elementary contraction of a path
\eta^{\prime}:I_{m+1}\to K
, there is some
0\leq i<m
, such that

•

\eta(j)=\eta^{\prime}(j)
for
j\leq i
;

•

\eta(j)=\eta(j+1)
for
j\geq i+1
; and

•

\eta^{\prime}(i)\eta^{\prime}(i+1)\eta^{\prime}(i+2)
spans a simplex in
K
.

Two edge paths
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
are edge homotopic if there is a finite sequence of edge paths
\eta=\eta_{0},\ldots,\eta_{k}=\eta^{\prime}
such that adjacent edge paths
\eta_{i}
and
\eta_{i+1}
in the sequence are related by an elementary contraction: either
\eta_{i}
is an elementary contraction of
\eta_{i+1}
or vice versa.

Edge homotopy is an equivalence relation on the set of edge paths on
K
. Like homotopy classes of paths on a topological space, homotopy classes of edge paths concatenate to form homotopy classes of edge paths. The homotopy class of the constant path (i.e. a sequence consisting of one vertex) functions as the identity, as concatenating an edge path with the constant path can be reduced to the edge path via an elementary contraction. Reversing the order of an edge path is equivalent to taking the inverse of the edge path: if we concatenate an edge path with one where the vertices are sequenced in reverse, elementary contractions can reduce it to the constant path.

Thus, taking vertices of
K
as objects and homotopy classes of edge paths between vertices as the set of morphisms, we can check that this gives us a groupoid.

Definition 2.7
.

The
edge groupoid

\mathcal{E}{{K}}
of a simplicial complex
K
is a groupoid whose objects are the set of vertices of
K
, and the set of morphisms
\mathcal{E}{{K}}(v,w)
from
v
to
w
is the set of homotopy classes of edge paths.

We call the vertex group
{e}\mathopen{}\left({{K,u}}\right)\mathclose{}
the
edge group
at
u
. Like the fundamental groupoid for the category of topological spaces, the edge groupoid is a functor
\mathcal{E}:\SCpx\to\Grpd
from the category of simplicial complexes to groupoids. If
f:L\to K
is a simplicial map, the we have an induced homomorphism of groupoids
\mathcal{E}{{f}}:\mathcal{E}{{L}}\to\mathcal{E}{{K}}
, since simplicial maps satisfy
eq.

1
. We refer the reader to
[
6
, §5.2]
and
[
1
, §6.4]
for details.

Because the set of morphisms of
\mathcal{E}{{K}}
are equivalence classes of edge paths, any morphism of
\mathcal{E}{{K}}(u_{0},u_{m})
can be expressed as a composition of the edge classes
[u_{0}u_{1}]\cdots[u_{m-1}u_{m}]
. It follows that any homomorphism of groupoids
F:\mathcal{E}{{K}}\to\mathsf{C}
can be fully expressed if we know
F
on its edges, as

F([u_{0}\cdots u_{m}])=F([u_{0}u_{1}])\cdots F([u_{m-1}u_{m}]).

The following lemma states that any groupoid homomorphism
F
from
\mathcal{E}{{K}}
can be defined by specifying its image on the edges, provided
F
satisfies the
cocycle condition

eq.

1
on two-simplices of
K
so that it is well-defined.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
K
be a simplicial complex and
\iota:K_{1}\hookrightarrow K
be the inclusion of its one-skeleton into
K
. Suppose we have a groupoid homomorphism
F_{1}:\mathcal{E}{{K_{1}}}\to\mathsf{C}
. Then there is a groupoid homomorphism
F:\mathcal{E}{{K}}\to\mathsf{C}
such that
F_{1}=F\circ\mathcal{E}{{\iota}}
, iff

\displaystyle F_{1}(uv)F_{1}(vw)F_{1}(wu)=\iden_{\mathsf{C}_{F_{1}(u)}}.

(1)

Proof.

Suppose we have
F:\mathcal{E}{{K}}\to\mathsf{C}
. For
uvw
spanning a simplex in
K
, since
[uv][vw][wu]=[uvw]=[u]
is edge-homotopic to the constant path by elementary collapses,
F
being a functor implies
F_{1}(uv)F_{1}(vw)F_{1}(wu)=F([uv])F([vw])F([wu])=F([uv][vw][wu])=F([u])=\iden
.

Conversely, suppose
eq.

1
are satisfied. We now verify that edge-homotopic paths from
x
and
y
are sent to the same morphism in
\mathsf{C}(F_{1}(x),F_{1}(y))
. Let the sequence of edge paths
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots a_{n}
be an edge homotopy. In other words, for adjacent edge paths in the sequence,
a_{i}
and
a_{i+1}
, either
a_{i}
contracts onto
a_{i+1}
or vice versa. In other words, we can write
a_{i}=b\cdot uvw\cdot c
and
a_{i+1}=b\cdot uw\cdot c
, or vice versa the collapse goes the other way. Applying
F_{1}
to the edge paths, and the identities in
eq.

1
, we have
F_{1}(a_{i})=F_{1}(b)F_{1}(uv)F_{1}(vw)F_{1}(c)=F_{1}(b)F_{1}(uw)F_{1}(c)=F_{1}(a_{i+1})
. Thus, we have a well-defined map between the set of morphisms
\mathcal{E}{{K}}(x,y)\to\mathsf{C}(F_{1}(x),F_{1}(y))
. Defining
F(x)=F_{1}(x)
, and
F([uv])=F_{1}(uv)
, we thus have a functor
F:\mathcal{E}{{K}}\to\mathsf{C}
such that
F_{1}=F\circ\mathcal{E}{{\iota}}
.
∎

2.2
Equivalence of the Fundamental and Edge Groupoids

We now show that the geometric realisation of a simplicial complex
K
induces a groupoid homomorphism
R:\mathcal{E}{{K}}\to\Pi{{K}}
. The homomorphism
R
formally relates homotopy classes of edge paths to homotopy classes of paths on the geometric realisation of a simplicial complex. We set up this result by considering a slightly more general category than that of simplicial complexes.

Recall a
good cover

\mathcal{U}=\left\{{U_{i}}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of a topological space is a collection of open sets
U_{i}
where nonempty finite intersections of cover elements are contractible. We further recall the concept of the
nerve
of an open cover. The nerve
\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}
of an open cover
\mathcal{U}
is the abstract simplicial complex on a vertex set indexed by
\mathcal{I}
, where

\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}=\left\{{J\subseteq\mathcal{I}}\ \mid\ {\cap_{j\in J}U_{j}\neq\emptyset\quad\&\quad|J|<\infty}\right\}.

(2)

Since we have restricted ourselves to good covers, the original nerve lemma of Leray
[
18
]
states that the geometric realisation of the nerve of a good cover is homotopy equivalent
X\simeq|\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}|
to the underlying space. We refer the reader to
[
3
,
2
]
for contemporary results where conditions on the cover are relaxed.

Example 2.9
.

Finite simplicial complexes dimension admit good covers. If we take the cover to be the collection of open vertex stars
\mathcal{V}=\left\{{\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({u}\right)\mathclose{}}\ \mid\ {u\in K_{0}}\right\}
, the nerve of the vertex stars is in fact isomorphic to
K
itself: an intersection of vertex stars
\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({u_{0}}\right)\mathclose{},\ldots,\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({u_{m}}\right)\mathclose{}
is non-empty precisely because their intersection contains the simplex
u_{0},\ldots,u_{m}
and any of its cofaces (i.e. its upper set in the face poset of
K
).

We now show that there is an equivalence of groupoids
\Pi{{X}}\simeq\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
if
\mathcal{U}
is a good cover. This is directly implied by the nerve lemma which proves that there exists a homotopy equivalence between
X\simeq|\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}|
. Here we explicitly construct the groupoid homomorphisms
R:\Pi{{X}}\to\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
and its adjoint
S:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\to\Pi{{X}}
, such that
R:\Pi{{X}}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}:S}}
is an equivalence of groupoids. We refer to
R
as the
realisation
homomorphism and
S
as the
snapping
homomorphism. These homomorphisms translate between homotopy classes of paths and edge paths. For paths in
X
, we can use the notion of
carriers

[
3
]
to discretise or ‘snap’ them to paths on the complex.

Definition 2.10
.

Given topological space
X
with a good cover
\mathcal{U}=\left\{{U_{i}}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
, we say an edge path
\eta:I_{m}\to\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}

carries
a path
\gamma:I\to X
, if the path can be parametrised such that for
k=0,\ldots,m

\displaystyle\textstyle{\gamma}\mathopen{}\left({\frac{k}{m}}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\in U_{\eta(k)}

(3)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\gamma}\mathopen{}\left({\left(\frac{k}{m},\frac{k+1}{m}\right)}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\subset U_{\eta(k)}\cup U_{\eta({k+1})},\quad k<m.

(4)

The proof of the following statements, which is a generalisation of the proofs of similar statements relating the edge group to the fundamental group in
[
1
, Theorem 6.10]
, is given in
Appendix

A
. Using the fact that any path in
X
is carried by some edge path on the nerve of a good cover, we can define the snapping morphism in
Proposition

2.12
. We defer the proofs to
Appendix

A
.

Lemma 2.11

(Path Carrier)
.

Let
X
be a space that admits an open cover
\mathcal{U}=\left\{{U_{i}}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
. If
x\in U_{i}
and
y\in U_{j}
, any path
\gamma:I\to X
from
x
to
y
is carried by some edge path on
\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}
from
i
to
j
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
X
be a space with a good cover
\mathcal{U}=\left\{{U_{i}}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
. Consider a pair of maps
r:\mathcal{I}\leftrightarrows X:s
, satisfying
r(i)\in U_{i}
and
x\in U_{s(x)}
. Then
r
and
s

uniquely
define a pair of groupoid homomorphisms
R:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\leftrightarrows\Pi{{X}}:S
, where on objects
r(u)=R(u)
and
S(x)=s(x)
; and on classes of paths:

•

R([\eta])\in\Pi{{X}}(r(i),r(j))
is the unique class of paths represented by a path
\gamma\in R([\eta])
carried by the edge path
\eta
; and

•

S([\gamma])\in\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}(s(x),s(y))
is the unique class of edge paths, one of which carries
\gamma\in[\gamma]\in\Pi{{X}}(x,y)
.

Furthermore, the pair
(R,S)
is an equivalence of categories between
\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}
and
\Pi{{X}}
. Explicitly, we have natural isomorphisms
\varsigma:\iden_{\Pi{{X}}}\Rightarrow RS
, and
\varrho:SR\Rightarrow\iden_{\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}}
. Furthermore,
\varsigma_{x}\in\Pi{{X}}(x,rs(x))
for any
x\in X
is a unique homotopy class of paths carried by
U_{S(x)}
, and
\varrho_{i}\in\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}(i,sr(i))
is the edge homotopy class of the edge from
i
to
sr(i)
on the nerve.

By considering
(|K|,\left\{{\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({u}\right)\mathclose{}}\right\}_{u\in K_{0}})
as a space with good cover,
Proposition

2.12
specialises to the following corollary. Note that we have a stronger condition on the choice of representatives of cover elements: a vertex star is represented by the vertex itself. Since the nerve of the vertex star cover is the simplicial complex itself, the edge groupoid here is the edge groupoid of the complex.

Corollary 2.13
.

Let
K
be a simplicial complex. We have an equivalence of categories
R:\mathcal{E}{{K}}\leftrightarrows\Pi{{K}}:S
, defined as follows:

•

On objects:
R
sends vertices on the abstract simplicial complex
K
to their corresponding points in its geometric realisation; and
S
snaps points in
x
to some vertex such that
x\in\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({S(x)}\right)\mathclose{}
. In particular, we can choose
SR(u)=u
;

•

On classes of paths,
R([\eta])=[|\eta|]\in\Pi{{X}}(r(i),r(j))
, and
S([\gamma])\in\mathcal{E}{{K}}(S(x),S(y))
is the class of edge paths edge-homotopic to one whose vertex stars carry
\gamma
.

Explicitly, we have natural isomorphisms
\varsigma:\iden_{\Pi{{K}}}\Rightarrow RS
, and
SR=\iden_{\mathcal{E}{{K}}}
. Furthermore,
\varsigma
is unique, and carried by
\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({S(x)}\right)\mathclose{}
.

Note that because each vertex is only contained in its own star and no others, the snapping map
s
must send
u\in|K|
to
u\in K
. Thus, if we choose
r
to send
u\in K
to
u\in|K|
, we must have
SR=\iden_{\mathcal{E}{{K}}}
.

We now show that the realisation functor
R:\mathcal{E}{{K}}\to\Pi{{K}}
is in fact a natural transformation of functors from the category of simplicial complexes to groupoids. Recall
\mathcal{E}:\SCpx\to\Grpd
is a functor from the category of simplicial complexes to groupoids. There is another functor,
\Pi:\SCpx\to\Grpd
, which is given by sending simplicial complexes to the fundamental groupoid of their geometric realisation. The following proposition shows that
R
is a natural transformation between these two functors.

Proposition 2.14
.

Consider the functors
\mathcal{E}:\SCpx\to\Grpd
and
\Pi:\SCpx\to\Grpd
from the category of simplicial complexes to groupoids. For any simplicial map
f:K\to L
, the realisation homomorphisms of
K
and
L
commute with the groupoid homomorphisms induced by
f
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}{{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi{{K}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}{{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi{{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}{{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi{{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{L}}

(5)

In other words, the realisation homomorphism is a natural transformation
R:\mathcal{E}\Rightarrow\Pi
.

Proof.

Recall the continuous map
|f|:|K|\to|L|
induced by a simplicial map
f
satisfies
|f|(|\sigma|)=|f(\sigma)|
. Furthermore, recall simplicial maps sends edge homotopic paths to homotopy classes of paths, and continuous maps sends homotopy classes of paths to homotopy classes of paths.

Consider an edge
vw\in K
, and the class
[vw]\in\mathcal{E}{{K}}(v,w)
. Following the top path of composition of maps in the square above,

[vw]\overset{R_{K}}{\longmapsto}[|vw|]\overset{\Pi{{f}}}{\longmapsto}[|f|(|vw|)]=[|f(v)f(w)|],

where the last equality is due to the property of the continuous maps induced by simplicial maps. Following the top path of composition of maps in the square above,

[vw]\overset{\mathcal{E}{{f}}}{\longmapsto}[f(v)f(w)]\overset{R_{L}}{\longmapsto}[|f(v)f(w)|].

Thus on the level of homotopy classes of edge paths represented by edges, the square commutes. Since any class of edge path is composed by homotopy classes of edge paths represented by edges, and
R_{\bullet}
,
\Pi
, and
\mathcal{E}
respects composition, the commutativity of the square for edges extends to commutativity of the square for any class of edge paths in
\mathcal{E}{{K}}
.
∎

While realisation is functorial, the snapping homomorphism
S
is not natural and depends on which vertices are chosen to represent points in
|K|
. However, the lemma below shows that snapping admits pullbacks: if
f:K\to L
is a simplicial map, for a given snapping
S_{L}:\Pi{{L}}\to\mathcal{E}{{L}}
, we can construct snapping
S_{K}:\Pi{{K}}\to\mathcal{E}{{K}}
, such that
eq.

6
commutes.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
f:K\to L
be a simplicial map. For any snapping homomorphism
S_{L}:\Pi{{L}}\to\mathcal{E}{{L}}
, we can find a snapping homomorphism
S_{K}:\Pi{{K}}\to\mathcal{E}{{K}}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\hbox to87.17pt{\vbox to44.34pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 43.49641pt\lower-19.1998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.63264pt}{-13.24164pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(6)

Proof.

We first proceed on the level of objects. Let
s_{L}:|L|\to L_{0}
be the snapping map that defines
S_{L}
. We show that we can find
s_{K}:|K|\to K_{0}
such that for all
x\in|K|
,

s_{L}\circ f(x)=f\circ s_{K}(x).

(7)

Let
\tau
be the unique simplex in
L
containing
f(x)
in its interior, and
s_{L}\circ f(x)=v
. Because
s_{L}
defines a snapping,
\tau
contains
v
in its boundary. If we examine preimages in
|K|
,
f^{-1}{\left({\tau}\right)}\ni x
, and the unique simplex
\sigma
in
K
containing
x
in its interior is in
f^{-1}{\left({\tau}\right)}
. Since every simplex in
f^{-1}{\left({\tau}\right)}
must have some vertex
u\in f^{-1}{\left({v}\right)}
in its boundary,
\sigma
must have some
u\in f^{-1}{\left({v}\right)}
in its boundary. In other words, there is some
u\in K_{0}
such that
x\in\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({u}\right)\mathclose{}
, and
f(u)=s_{L}\circ f(x)
. We define
s_{K}(x)
by picking one such vertex
u=s_{K}(x)
to be the image of
x
. From
Proposition

2.12

s_{K}
uniquely defines a snapping homomorphism
S:\Pi{{X}}\to\mathcal{E}{{K}}
.

Having established
eq.

7
which shows that
Equation

6
commutes on the level of objects, we now compare how
S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}
and
\mathcal{E}{{f}}\circ S_{K}
sends homotopy classes of paths. Since
SR
is the identity functor, and
R_{L}\circ\mathcal{E}{{f}}=\Pi{{f}}\circ R_{K}
, we have

\mathcal{E}{{f}}\circ S_{K}=S_{L}R_{L}\circ\mathcal{E}{{f}}\circ S_{K}=S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}\circ R_{K}S_{K}.

Thus
eq.

6
commutes iff
S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}\circ R_{K}S_{K}=S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}
.

From
Proposition

2.12
, we have a unique natural transformation
\varsigma^{K}:\iden_{\Pi{{K}}}\Rightarrow R_{K}S_{K}
. Consider
[\gamma]\in\Pi{{K}}(x,y)
. Then

S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}\circ R_{K}S_{K}([\gamma])=(S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}(\varsigma_{x}^{K}))^{-1}\ast(S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}([\gamma]))\ast(S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}(\varsigma_{y}^{K})).

(8)

Consider
S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}(\varsigma_{x}^{K})
. Recall
\varsigma_{x}^{K}
is the unique class of paths from
x
to
S_{K}(x)
carried by
\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({S_{K}(x)}\right)\mathclose{}
. Since
f(\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({S_{K}(x)}\right)\mathclose{})\subseteq\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({f\circ S_{K}(x)}\right)\mathclose{}=\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({S_{L}\circ f(x)}\right)\mathclose{}
, the class of paths
\varsigma_{x}^{K}
is mapped by
\Pi{{f}}
to a class of paths
\Pi{{X}}(f(x),R_{L}S_{L}(f(x)))
carried by
\mathstar\mathopen{}\left({S_{L}\circ f(x)}\right)\mathclose{}
. In other words,
\Pi{{f}}(\varsigma_{x}^{K})=\varsigma_{f(x)}^{L}
. Thus,
S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}(\varsigma_{x}^{K})=(\varsigma_{f(x)}^{L})^{-1}\varsigma_{f(x)}^{L}=e\in{\pi_{1}}({L,S_{L}\circ f(x)})
. Substituting into the equation above, we thus have
S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}\circ R_{K}S_{K}=S_{L}\circ\Pi{{f}}
.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

We emphasise that snappings are not natural transformations. Since the snapping
S_{K}
is constructed so that it is compatible with the snapping
S_{L}
in the codomain, if we have multiple simplicial maps
f_{i}:K\to L_{i}
, there may not be a snapping
S_{K}
that is compatible with all of
S_{L_{i}}
. The example in
Figure

1
illustrates how there is no choice of snappings
S_{L_{i}}
that admit a compatible snapping
S_{K}
.

Figure 1:
Consider the boundary of a two-simplex
K
and the three possible surjective simplicial maps from
K
to the closure of edge. The images of the maps on vertices is marked out in the diagram. For the (arbitrarily chosen) snapping on the left hand side edge, we illustrate the only snapping on the bottom edge, such that there is a snapping on
K
that is simultaneously compatible with snappings on both the left and bottom edge. There is no snapping on the remaining edge that is compatible with this snapping. In other words, there is no choice of snappings on the three codomains, where there is a snapping on the pre-image such that
eq.

6
commutes for all three maps.

2.3
Homology

The concept of group homology extends to that of groupoids. We refer the reader to
[
25
, Ch.6]
for a comprehensive account of group homology, and give an elementary account of the basics of groupoid homology, deferring details to
[
14
, Ch.16]
.

We first define the notion of a
simplex
of the groupoid
\mathsf{C}
. Recall the simplicial groupoid
\Delta^{n}

2.2
. An
n
-simplex of
\mathsf{C}
is a groupoid homomorphism
\sigma:\Delta^{n}\to\mathsf{C}
; we denote the set of
n
-simplices of
\mathsf{C}
as
\Sigma_{n}(G)
. In particular,
\Sigma_{0}(\mathsf{C})
is the set of objects of
\mathsf{C}
;
\Sigma_{1}(\mathsf{C})
is the set of morphisms of
\mathsf{C}
; and
\Sigma_{2}(\mathsf{C})
is the set of triplets of morphisms
(f,g,h)
of
\mathsf{C}
satisfying
h=fg
. Denoting
\phi_{k}^{n}:\Delta^{n-1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}
to be the groupoid monomorphism including the
k
th
face of
\Delta^{n}
into
\Delta^{n}
(that is, the image is the simplex with the
k
th
object deleted), we induce a function
\psi_{k}^{n}:\Sigma_{n}\to\Sigma_{n-1}
where
\sigma\mapsto\sigma\circ\phi_{k}^{n}
. A groupoid homomorphism
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
induces a function
\Sigma_{k}(\mathsf{C})\to\Sigma_{k}(\mathsf{D})
where
\sigma:\Delta^{n}\to\mathsf{C}
is sent to a simplex
F\circ\sigma:\Delta^{n}\to\mathsf{D}
.

We fix an abelian group
A
to be the homology coefficient group. We define the
k
-chains of
\mathsf{C}
with
A
-coefficients to be the direct sum over the abelian groups
A
indexed over the
k
-simplices
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathsf{C})
:

{\mathcal{C}_{k}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C};M}\right)\mathclose{}:=\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}(\mathsf{C})}A.

(9)

The face relations induce the boundary maps
\partial_{k}:{\mathcal{C}_{k}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C};M}\right)\mathclose{}\to{\mathcal{C}_{k-1}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C};M}\right)\mathclose{}
that is the linear extension of

\partial\sigma=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\psi_{k}^{n}\sigma.

(10)

We can check via standard arguments that
\partial^{2}=0
. We then define the
k
th
homology of the groupoid
{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C};M}\right)\mathclose{}
with
A
coefficients as the chain complex of
({\mathcal{C}_{k}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C};M}\right)\mathclose{},\partial)
.

Passing from groupoids to homology is functorial. A groupoid homomorphism
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
induces morphisms of complexes
{\mathcal{C}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C};A}\right)\mathclose{}\to{\mathcal{C}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{D};A}\right)\mathclose{}
, and homomorphism of homology groups
{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({F;A}\right)\mathclose{}:{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C};A}\right)\mathclose{}\to{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{D};A}\right)\mathclose{}
.
Furthermore, if
\theta:F\Rightarrow G
is a homotopy of groupoid morphisms, then we induce identical homomorphisms
{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({F;A}\right)\mathclose{}={\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({G;A}\right)\mathclose{}

[
14
, Theorem 15]
. As a consequence, an equivalence of groupoids
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
induces an isomorphism
{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C};A}\right)\mathclose{}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{D};A}\right)\mathclose{}
.

We collect some further facts about the homology of groupoids. Here on we suppress the coefficient group
A
in our notation. First, if
\mathsf{C}
has connected components
\mathsf{C}^{(i)}
, then
{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}=\bigoplus_{i}{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C}^{(i)}}\right)\mathclose{}
; if
\mathsf{C}
is connected, and
\mathsf{C}_{v}
is a vertex group of
\mathsf{C}
, then the equivalence of groupoids between a connected groupoid and any of its vertex groups implies
{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C}_{v}}\right)\mathclose{}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C}}\right)\mathclose{}
. Since the groupoid homology of a group
G
is its group homology, for a connected groupoid
\mathsf{C}
we thus have
{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C};A}\right)\mathclose{}\cong A
and
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{C};A}\right)\mathclose{}\cong{\mathsf{C}_{v}}/[\mathsf{C}_{v},\mathsf{C}_{v}]\otimes_{\Z}A
due to
[
25
, Theorem 6.1.12]
. We reproduce an elementary proof in
Appendix

B
for completeness.

Proposition 2.17
.

If
G
is a group, and
A
is an abelian group, then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\mathopen{}\left({G;A}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\cong A

(11)

\displaystyle\eta:{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({G;A}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}{G}^{\mathsf{ab}}\otimes A.

(12)

The isomorphism
\eta
is evaluated on a representative element of a class of
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({G;A}\right)\mathclose{}
as

\textstyle\eta:\left[\sum_{i}\langle g_{i}\rangle\otimes a_{i}\right]\mapsto\sum_{i}g_{i}\otimes a_{i},

(13)

where
\langle g\rangle
to denote elements of
G
as a basis for the free abelian group
{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({G}\right)\mathclose{}=\Z[G]
; the left hand side sum is a formal sum, while the right hand side sum denotes addition in the abelian group
{G}^{\mathsf{ab}}\otimes A
.

In the context of this paper, we are mainly concerned with relating the homology of the fundamental groupoid of a space with the singular homology of the underlying space, we restrict our treatment here to homology up to degree one, as the fundamental groupoid retains only the one-dimensional topology of the underlying space. Due to the Hurewicz homomorphism
[
13
, §2.A]

{{\pi_{1}}({X,x})}^{\mathsf{ab}}\cong H_{1}(X)
for the singular homology of path-connected spaces, and
{{e}\mathopen{}\left({{K,u}}\right)\mathclose{}}^{\mathsf{ab}}\cong H_{1}(K)

[
24
, §5.1.3]
for the first simplicial homology group of a path-connected complex, we obtain the following result by considering the vertex groups of the fundamental and edge complexes: for
k=0,1
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\mathopen{}\left({\Pi{{X}};A}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\cong H_{k}(X;A)

(14)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{E}{{K}};A}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\cong H_{k}(K;A).

(15)

Because we have an equivalence of groupoids between the edge groupoid of a simplicial complex and the fundamental groupoid of its geometric realisation, this also implies the equivalence of singular and simplicial homology in the special cases of degree = 0,1.

We show below that regarding
{H}_{\bullet}\circ\Pi
and
H_{\bullet}
as functors from topological spaces to abelian groups, we have a natural isomorphism between them.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
X
be a topological space. The function
\gamma\mapsto[\gamma]
sending paths to their homotopy class rel end points, induces for
k=0,1
isomorphisms
{s}_{k}:H_{k}(X;A)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\mathopen{}\left({\Pi{{X}};A}\right)\mathclose{}
, given on representative cycles by

\displaystyle{s}_{0}:\sum_{i}x_{i}\otimes a_{i}+B_{0}(X;A)

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i}x_{i}\otimes a_{i}+\mathcal{B}_{0}({\Pi{{X}};A});

(16)

\displaystyle{s}_{1}:\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\otimes a_{i}+B_{1}(X;A)

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i}[\sigma_{i}]\otimes a_{i}+\mathcal{B}_{1}({\Pi{{X}};A}).

(17)

Proof.

{s}
from a chain homomorphism

(18)

We first consider the case where
A=\Z
. As a short hand, we let
B_{1}=B_{1}(X)
,
C_{1}=C_{1}(X)
, and
\mathcal{B}_{1}={\mathcal{C}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({\Pi{{X}}}\right)\mathclose{}
, and
\mathcal{B}_{1}=\mathcal{B}_{1}({\Pi{{X}}})
.

We first show that
C_{1}/B_{1}\cong\mathcal{B}_{1}/\mathcal{B}_{1}

Let
q:C_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{1}
be the unique homomorphism between the free abelian groups induced by the function
\gamma\mapsto[\gamma]
. Explicitly,

q:\sum_{i}n_{i}\sigma_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i}n_{i}[\sigma_{i}]

where
\sigma_{i}
are singular one-simplices. We show that
\ker q\subset B
. Let us write any element of
C_{1}
as
c=(-1)^{k_{1}}\sigma_{1}+\cdots+(-1)^{k_{m}}\sigma_{m}
, where
\sigma_{i}
are allowed to repeat. If
q(c)=0
, then terms in the formal sum can be paired and indexed such that
\sigma_{2k}\simeq\sigma_{2k+1}
and they have oppositely signed coefficients. Let us then write
c
as
c=(-1)^{k_{1}}(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})+\cdots+(-1)^{k_{m-1}}(\sigma_{m-1}-\sigma_{m})
. Since
\sigma_{2k}\simeq\sigma_{2k+1}
, each
(\sigma_{2j}-\sigma_{2j+1})
term in the sum is a boundary of a singular two-simplex (see
[
13
]
, theorem 2A.1, points
(i-ii)
of proof). Thus,
\ker q\subset B
.

We first consider the following homomorphisms between such groups

\hbox to152.9pt{\vbox to44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 79.36429pt\lower-22.00005pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-76.53265pt}{-19.50005pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

where the homomorphism
q
sends simplicial chains
\sum_{i}c_{i}\sigma_{i}
to the formal sum of homotopy classes of paths
\sum_{i}c_{i}[\sigma_{i}]
. We proceed to verify that the homomorphisms
q\rvert_{B}
and
q^{\ast}
– the induced homomorphisms on quotients – exist, and commute with the other homomorphisms in the diagram.

Let us first consider
q\rvert_{B}
and check that
q(B_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{1}
. Consider the boundary of singular two-simplex
a+b-c
. Since
c\simeq a\cdot b
, we have
q(a+b-c)=[a]+[b]-[a\cdot b]=[a]+[b]-[a]\cdot[b]\in\mathcal{B}
. Extending linearly to boundary chains shows that
q(B_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{1}
. As such
q
induces a homomorphism
q^{\ast}:C_{1}/B_{1}\to{C_{1}/B_{1}}
. Moreover,
q\rvert_{B}
is a surjection onto
\mathcal{B}_{1}
, since every
[a]+[b]-[a\cdot b]
is the image of a boundary of a two-simplex (see
[
13
]
, proof of theorem 2A.1, item
(iii)
). As such,
q(B_{1})=\mathcal{B}_{1}
, and the diagram above commutes. This implies
q^{\ast}
is injective. Because
q
is surjective, we thus conclude that
q^{\ast}
is an isomorphism.

If we consider the zero chain groups, we have an isomorphism
C_{0}(X)={\mathcal{C}_{0}}\mathopen{}\left({\Pi{{X}}}\right)\mathclose{}
, as they are free abelian groups over the same basis. From the definition of the first boundary homomorphism of either chains, which sends (the class of) path
\gamma
to
\gamma(1)-\gamma(0)
, the following commutes and thus we have a chain homomorphism

Because the right hand side square commutes and
q
is surjective, we have an isomorphism
{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\mathopen{}\left({X}\right)\mathclose{}\cong H_{0}(X)
. Furthermore, since
q^{\ast}:C_{1}/B_{1}\to{C_{1}/B_{1}}
is an isomorphism, the diagram above implies an isomorphism
{s}:H_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({\Pi{{X}}}\right)\mathclose{}
between the kernels of
\partial_{1}
restricted to the quotient groups.

Having taken care of the case
A=\Z
, we consider coefficients in arbitrary abelian groups. Taking tensor products of the diagram above with
\otimes A
yield the corresponding homomorphisms for the chains in
A
-coefficients. Because the universal coefficient theorem in degree
k\leq 1
implies there are natural homomorphisms
H_{k}(C_{\bullet})\otimes A\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{k}(C_{\bullet};A)
for
C_{\bullet}
a chain complex of free abelian groups, the induced homomorphisms
{s}_{k}:H_{k}(X;A)\to{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\mathopen{}\left({\Pi{{X}};A}\right)\mathclose{}
is an isomorphism for all abelian groups
A
.
∎

Proposition 2.19
.

For
k=0,1
, there is a natural isomorphism between the singular homology functor
H_{k}:\TopS\to\AbGrp
and
{H}_{k}\circ\Pi:\TopS\xrightarrow{\Pi}\Grpd\xrightarrow{{H}_{k}}\AbGrp
. Concretely, for a continuous map
f:X\to Y
between topological spaces, the isomorphisms
{s}_{k}
given in
Lemma

2.18
commute with the induced homomorphisms below:

(19)

Proof.

We show this from direct evaluation. For
k=1
, and a representative cycle
h=\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\otimes a_{i}+B_{1}(X;A)
,

\displaystyle{s}_{1}^{Y}\circ H_{1}(f)(h)

\displaystyle={s}_{1}^{Y}(\sum_{i}(f\circ\sigma_{i})\otimes a_{i}+B_{1}(Y;A))=\sum_{i}[f\circ\sigma_{i}]\otimes a_{i}+B_{1}(\Pi{{Y}};A)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({\Pi{{f}}}\right)\mathclose{}\circ{s}_{1}^{X}(h)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{H}_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({\Pi{{f}}}\right)\mathclose{}(\sum_{i}[\sigma_{i}]\otimes a_{i}+B_{1}(\Pi{{X}};A))=\sum_{i}[f\circ\sigma_{i}]\otimes a_{i}+B_{1}(\Pi{{Y}};A)

\displaystyle={s}_{1}^{Y}\circ H_{1}(f)(h).

The case for
k=0
follows along similar lines upon direct evaluation of such homomorphisms.
∎

We show similar results for the edge groupoid.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
K
be a simplicial complex. The function
uv\mapsto[uv]
sending edges to their homotopy class of edge paths rel end points, , induces for
k=0,1
isomorphisms from simplicial homology groups
{s}_{k}:H_{k}(K;A)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{E}{{K}};A}\right)\mathclose{}
; they are given on representative cycles by

\displaystyle{s}_{0}:\sum_{i}v_{i}\otimes a_{i}+B_{0}(K;A)

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i}v_{i}\otimes a_{i}+\mathcal{B}_{0}({\mathcal{E}{{K}};A});

(20)

\displaystyle{s}_{1}:\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\otimes a_{i}+B_{1}(K;A)

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i}[\sigma_{i}]\otimes a_{i}+\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathcal{E}{{K}};A).

(21)

Proof.

In a similar vein to
Lemma

2.18
, we derive the isomorphisms
{s}
from a chain homomorphism. Note however that there are subtle differences in the chain homomorphisms: the singular chains surject onto
{\mathcal{C}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\Pi{{X}}}\right)\mathclose{}
, while the simplicial chains inject into
{\mathcal{C}_{\bullet}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{E}{{K}}}\right)\mathclose{}
.

(22)

We first consider the case where
A=\Z
. As a short hand, we let
B=B_{1}(K)
,
C=C_{1}(K)
, and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{E}{{X}})
, and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E}{{K}})
. Let
q:C\to\mathcal{C}
be the unique homomorphism between the free abelian groups induced by the function
uv\mapsto[uv]
sending edges to their homotopy class of paths. We first show that
q(B)\subseteq\mathcal{B}
, and thus we induce a homomorphism
q^{\ast}:C/B\to\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{B}
. Each element in
B
is a linear combination over elements
uv+vw-uw
where
uvw
is a two-simplex in
K
. Because

q(uv+vw-uw)=\langle[uv]\rangle+\langle[vw]\rangle-\langle[uw]\rangle=\langle[uv]\rangle+\langle[vw]\rangle-\langle[uv]\cdot[vw]\rangle\in\mathcal{B},

we thus have
q(B)\subseteq\mathcal{B}
. In addition,
q^{\ast}
is surjective: because
\langle[f]\cdot[g]\rangle\sim\langle[f]\rangle+\langle[g]\rangle
in
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{B}
, and every homotopy class of edge path can be expressed as a composition of classes of edges, any element of
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{B}
is represented by a formal sum over edge classes
\langle[uv]\rangle
. Suppose the orientation of the edge class
\langle[vu]\rangle
is opposite to the fixed orientation of the edge
uv
as a basis element of
C
. Then we have
\langle[vu]\rangle+\mathcal{B}=-\langle[uv]\rangle+\mathcal{B}
, because

\langle[uv]\rangle+\langle[vu]\rangle-\langle[uvu]\rangle\in\mathcal{B}\quad\text{and}\quad\langle[uvu]\rangle=\langle[u]\rangle=\langle[u]\rangle+\langle[u]\rangle-\langle[u]\cdot[u]\rangle\in\mathcal{B}.

We can thus express any element of
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{B}
as the image of some formal sum over a basis of oriented edges. Therefore
q^{\ast}
is surjective.

Finally, to show that
q^{\ast}
is an isomorphism, we prove that it is also injective. In other words, if a one-chain
c\in C
is mapped to
q(c)\in\mathcal{B}
, then
c\in B
. We construct a homomorphism
p:\mathcal{B}\to C
between the free abelian groups by specifying the function between basis elements: for each basis element
[f]\in\mathcal{B}
corresponding to an edge path, we choose a fixed representative edge path
v_{0}\cdots v_{m}\in[f]
, and send it to
p(\langle[f]\rangle)=v_{0}v_{1}+\cdots v_{m-1}v_{m}
. Consider then the elements in
\mathcal{B}
. We have
p(\mathcal{B})\subseteq B
, because each element
\langle[f]\rangle+\langle[g]\rangle-\langle[f\cdot g]\rangle
in the spanning set of

\mathcal{B}
is sent to a chain
a_{0}a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n-1}a_{n}+a_{n}a_{0}
such that
a_{0}a_{1}\cdots a_{n}a_{0}
is a null-homotopic loop. We recall if an edge loop
a_{0}a_{1}\cdots a_{m}
is null-homotopic, then the chain
a_{0}a_{1}+\cdots+a_{m-1}a_{m}+a_{m}a_{0}
is in
B
(see
[
1
, p.182]
). Hence,
a_{0}a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n-1}a_{n}+a_{n}a_{0}
is in
B
and
p(\mathcal{B})\subseteq B
.

We now show that
q(c)\in\mathcal{B}
implies
c\in B
, by showing that
p\circ q
is the identity on
B
. The homomorphism
p
depends on the choice of representative of edge paths. For a class of edge paths homotopy equivalent to an edge path
uv
, we always choose the edge
uv
to be the representative path. Thus, for
c=\sum_{i}n_{i}\sigma_{i}\in q^{-1}{\left({\mathcal{B}}\right)}
,

\textstyle p\circ q(c)={p}\mathopen{}\left({\sum_{i}n_{i}\langle[\sigma_{i}]\rangle}\right)\mathclose{}=\sum_{i}n_{i}\sigma_{i}=c\in\imag p\subseteq B.

Hence
q(c)\in\mathcal{B}
implies
c\in B
. Thus
q^{\ast}
is injective and therefore an isomorphism.

If we consider the zero-chain groups, we have an isomorphism
C_{0}(K)\cong{\mathcal{C}_{0}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{E}{{K}}}\right)\mathclose{}
, as they are both free abelian groups. The right hand side square of
eq.

22
commutes:
\partial_{1}\circ q(uv)=\partial_{1}[uv]=u-v
. Because
q^{\ast}
is also an isomorphism, the image and kernels of the respective boundary homomorphisms on the top and bottom chains are isomorphic, and thus
q
induces the isomorphism
{s}_{k}:H_{k}(K)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{E}{{K}}}\right)\mathclose{}
for
k=0,1
.

In the case for arbitrary coefficients, the same homological algebra arguments used in the proof of
Lemma

2.18
apply to arrive at the same statements.
∎

Proposition 2.21
.

For
k=0,1
, there is a natural isomorphism between the simplicial homology functor
H_{k}:\SCpx\to\AbGrp
and
{H}_{k}\circ\mathcal{E}:\SCpx\xrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}\Grpd\xrightarrow{{H}_{k}}\AbGrp
. Concretely, for a simplicial map
f:K\to L
, the isomorphisms
{s}_{k}
given in
Lemma

2.20
commute with the induced homomorphisms below:

(23)

Proof.

This can be verified via explicit computation, as in
Proposition

2.19
.
∎

While passing the homology of the fundamental or edge groupoid does not encode more information about the underlying space than that of singular or simplicial homology, we make the equivalence plain as we deal with how groupoid homomorphisms induce homomorphisms on the level of homology. In particular, when we discuss
\Gamma
-coverings (where
\Gamma
is a discrete group) of a space
X
in the subsequent section, the monodromy of the covering can be encoded as a groupoid homomorphism
\mu:\Pi{{X}}\to\Gamma
. If the base space
X
of the covering admits a good cover
\mathcal{U}
, then the atlas of the bundle can be equivalently encoded as a groupoid homomorphism
t:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\to\Gamma
. Applying the functor
{H}_{\bullet}:\Grpd\to\AbGrp
, we show that the monodromy and atlas homomorphism
\mu
and
t
respectively induce homomorphisms on singular homology
\mu^{\ast}:H_{1}(X;A)\to{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{ab}}\otimes A
and simplicial homology
t^{\ast}:H_{1}(K;A)\to{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{ab}}\otimes A

\displaystyle\mu^{\ast}:\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\otimes a_{i}+B_{1}(X;A)\mapsto\sum_{i}\mu([\sigma_{i}])\otimes a_{i}

\displaystyle t^{\ast}:\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\otimes a_{i}+B_{1}(K;A)\mapsto\sum_{i}t([\sigma_{i}])\otimes a_{i}.

3
Covering Spaces

We refer the reader to
[
13
]
for the details of the facts about covering spaces summarised below. Recall a
covering
of a topological space
X
is a continuous surjection
p:\tilde{X}\twoheadrightarrow X
, such that for each neighbourhood of
x\in X
, there is an open neighbourhood
U
where

p^{-1}{\left({U}\right)}=\bigsqcup_{\tilde{x}\in p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)}}U_{\tilde{x}},

(24)

and the restriction of
p
to each
U_{\tilde{x}}
is a homeomorphism onto
U
.

We focus on the following special case of coverings
p:\tilde{X}\twoheadrightarrow X
.

Definition 3.1
.

A continuous surjection
p:\tilde{X}\twoheadrightarrow X
is a
\Gamma
-covering, if the following conditions are satisfied:

(C1)

X
is locally path connected and semi-locally simply connected topological spaces;

(C2)

p
is the quotient of
\tilde{X}
by a

covering space action
of a discrete group
\Gamma
on
\tilde{X}
.

We recall a free group action
\Gamma\times Y\to Y
is a covering space action
[
13
]

3
3

3

a.k.a. a properly discontinuous action.
, if for any point
y\in Y
, there is a neighbourhood
V
such that

(g\cdot V)\cap V\neq\emptyset\quad\implies\quad g=e.

(25)

Since the covering map is the quotient of such an action,
U=p(V)
is evenly covered, and
p^{-1}{\left({U}\right)}
is simply the orbit
\Gamma\cdot V
. Thus for
U
evenly covered, we have a
local trivialisation

(26)

where
\varphi_{U}
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant map, which we call a
chart
; if
\varphi_{U}(z)=(x,g)
, then

\varphi_{U}(h\cdot z)=h\cdot\varphi_{U}(z)=(x,hg).

(27)

Because
\varphi_{U}
is equivariant,
\varphi_{U}
is uniquely defined by where
\varphi_{U}^{-1}
sends
U\times\left\{{e}\right\}
. If
\varphi_{U}^{\prime}
is another local trivialisation, then equivariance implies there is some
\theta\in\Gamma
such that
\varphi_{U}^{\prime}\circ\varphi_{U}^{-1}(x,g)=(x,g\cdot\theta)
for all
x\in U
. Note that
\theta
does not depend on
x
as
\Gamma
is discrete.

We now consider the group of automorphisms of coverings. If
p,q:\tilde{X}_{1}\to X
are coverings of
X
, then we say a homeomorphism
f:\tilde{X}\to\tilde{X}
is an isomorphism of coverings
p
and
q
if
q=\tilde{f}p
. These homeomorphisms form the group of
deck transformations

\mathrm{Aut}({p})
of the covering
p
. For
p
a
\Gamma
-covering, it follows from the definition that
\Gamma
is a subgroup of deck transformations, and furthermore, if the covering is path-connected, then
\Gamma
is the group of deck transformations
[
13
, Prop 1.40]
.

An important class of
\Gamma
-covering spaces are
universal coverings

p:\tilde{X}\to X
, those where the covering space
\tilde{X}
is simply connected. A path-connected space
X
admits a universal covering iff
(C1)
is satisfied. In that case, we have an isomorphism
{\pi_{1}}({{X},{x}})\cong\mathrm{Aut}({p})\cong\Gamma
mediated by homotopy path lifting, which we discuss below. We give some classical examples of universal coverings of manifolds.

Example 3.2
.

We consider four simple examples of
\Gamma
-coverings that are universal.

(i)

The universal covering
p:\R^{2}\to\mathbb{T}
of the flat torus by
\R^{2}
is a
\Gamma=\Z\oplus\Z
covering; the group action is given by

(n,m)\cdot(x,y)\mapsto(x+n,y+m).

(ii)

The universal covering
p:\R^{2}\to\mathbb{K}
of the flat Klein bottle by
\R^{2}
is a
\Gamma=\Z\rtimes\Z
covering; the group action is given by

(n,m)\cdot(x,y)\mapsto((-1)^{m}x+n,y+m).

(iii)

The universal covering
p:\Sbb^{n}\to\RP^{n}
of the real projective space by
\Sbb^{2}
is a
\Gamma=\Z/2\Z
covering; the group action is given by

a\cdot x\mapsto(-1)^{a}x

where a point
x\in\Sbb^{n}
here is regarded as a unit vector in
\R^{n+1}
.

(iv)

The universal covering
p:\mathbb{D}\to\Sigma_{2}
of the compact surface of genus 2 by the hyperbolic plane is a
\Gamma=\langle g_{0},g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\mid g_{0}g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}g_{3}^{-1}g_{0}^{-1}g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}g_{3}\rangle
covering; using the Poincaré disk model
\mathbb{D}=\left\{{z\in\mathbb{C}}\ \mid\ {|z|<1}\right\}
as the model for the hyperbolic plane, the group action on
\mathbb{D}
is a Möbius transformation on the complex plane, where the individual action of a generator
g_{k}
is given in matrix form by

g_{k}\cdot z=\frac{z+\omega^{k}\sqrt{1-c^{2}}}{\omega^{-k}\sqrt{1-c^{2}}z+1},

where
c=\tan(\pi/8)
, and
\omega=e^{i\pi/4}
.

3.1
Monodromy

Since covering spaces are fibre bundles and thus have the homotopy lifting property with respect to discs
[
13
, Prop 4.48]
, the homotopy groups of
\tilde{X}
and
X
are related via that of the fibre of the projection by a long exact sequence
[
13
, Theorem 4.41]
if
X
is path-connected. In particular, since the fibres of covering spaces are discrete, the long exact sequence breaks apart into the following exact sequences: for homotopy groups
\pi_{n}
with
n\geq 2
, the covering map induces isomorphisms
\pi_{n}(p):\pi_{n}(\tilde{X},\tilde{x})\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{n}(X,x)
; and for
n\leq 1
,

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(28)

Since
p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)}
is discrete, we have a bijection between
p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)}
and
\pi_{0}(p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)},\tilde{x})
. Note that since
\pi_{0}
is the pointed set of path-connected components rather than a group, ‘exactness’ of this sequence should be understood in terms of maps between pointed sets:

•

The map
\delta
takes a class of loops to a point in
p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)}
, that is path-connected with
\tilde{x}
in
\tilde{X}
;

•

The injection of loops in
{\pi_{1}}({\tilde{X},\tilde{x}})
into
{\pi_{1}}({X,x})
are the classes of loops
{\pi_{1}}({X,x})
that are sent to
\tilde{x}
by
\delta
;

•

Furthermore, the path connected components of
\tilde{X}
are enumerated by points of the fibre
p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)}
, modulo equivalence by being in the image of
\delta
.

Due to the exactness of
eq.

28
, the homotopy groups of the covering space can be recovered by knowing the map
\delta
; because
{\pi_{1}}({p})
is a monomorphism,
{\pi_{1}}({\tilde{X}})
can be recovered from the kernel of
\delta
, whereas
\pi_{0}(\tilde{X})
can be recovered from the image of
\delta
in
\pi_{0}(p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)})
. The higher homotopy groups are isomorphic to that of
X
itself.

In the case of a
\Gamma
-covering, since
\Gamma
acts freely on fibres, we have a bijection between
\pi_{0}(p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)})
and
\Gamma
as sets. In fact, the set map
\delta
can be refined as a group homomorphism
\mu
between
\pi_{1}(X)
and
\Gamma
. We call
\mu
the
monodromy homomorphism
. We define a generalisation of this group homomorphism to a groupoid homomorphism from
\Pi{{X}}
to
\Gamma
in
Definition

3.3
. In
Proposition

3.6
and
Proposition

3.8
, we refine the observations we have surmised from
eq.

28
in group theoretic terms. We first derive the monodromy homomorphism via homotopy lifting, and demonstrate some relevant properties.

The monodromy homomorphism is constructed by homotopy lifting of paths. We recall the following elementary fact of covering spaces and path lifting. Each path
\gamma:(I,0)\to(X,x_{0})
has a unique lift
\tilde{\gamma}:(I,0)\to(\tilde{X},\tilde{x}_{0})
which commutes with the projection
\gamma=p\circ\tilde{\gamma}
. Furthermore, the lift respects homotopy: the homotopy class of paths
[\gamma]
based at
x_{0}
lifts to a homotopy class of paths
[\tilde{\gamma}]
based at
\tilde{x}_{0}
. In particular, if
[\gamma]
is a class of loops based at
x
, then
[\gamma]
lifts to a unique homotopy class of paths
[\tilde{\gamma}]
with fixed end points in
\tilde{x}_{0},\tilde{x}_{1}\in p^{-1}{\left({x_{0}}\right)}
. Furthermore, classes of loops that lift to loops (i.e.
\tilde{x}_{1}=\tilde{x}_{0}
) form precisely the subgroup of
{\pi_{1}}({{X},{x_{0}}})
that is the image of the induced map
p_{\ast}:{\pi_{1}}({{\tilde{X}},{\tilde{x}_{0}}})\to{\pi_{1}}({{X},{x_{0}}})
.

We can describe path lifting in terms of the fundamental groupoids of the covering spaces. For
x\in X
, let
\mathstar_{X}x
denote the classes of paths in the fundamental groupoid with
x
as the source

\mathstar_{X}x:=\bigcup_{y\in X}\Pi{{X}}(x,y).

(29)

Fixing a point
\tilde{x}\in p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)}
, the homotopy lifting of paths can be regarded as a map
\hslash_{\tilde{x}}:\mathstar_{X}x\to\mathstar_{\tilde{X}}\tilde{x}
, where
\hslash_{\tilde{x}}([\gamma])
is the unique element of
\mathstar\tilde{x}
such that

\Pi{{p}}\circ\hslash_{\tilde{x}}([\gamma])=[\gamma].

(30)

The lifting map
\hslash_{\tilde{x}}
is a bijection
[
4
, 10.2.1]
: any path
\tilde{\gamma}
in
\tilde{X}
based at
\tilde{x}
projects onto a path
\gamma
that can only lift to
\tilde{\gamma}
for paths based at
\tilde{\gamma}
; and if two paths with the same end points are lifted to the same class of paths in
\tilde{X}
, then the projection of the homotopy between the two lifted paths rel end points is a homotopy of the initial two paths in
X
rel end points. Thus
\hslash_{\tilde{x}}
is surjective and injective.

For
p
a
\Gamma
-covering, since the group action on
\tilde{X}
acts freely on the fibre of points, we can specify
\tilde{\gamma}(1)\in p^{-1}{\left({y}\right)}
of a lifted path by the unique group element
g
such that for a given fibre reference point
\lambda(y)\in p^{-1}{\left({y}\right)}
, we have
\tilde{\gamma}(1)=g\cdot\lambda(y)
. Since all paths in
[\gamma]\in\Pi{{X}}(x,y)
lift to the same class
[\tilde{\gamma}]\in\Pi{{\tilde{X}}}(\lambda(x),g\cdot\lambda(y))
, we have a well-defined map from
\Pi{{X}}(x,y)
to
\Gamma
. Note that this depends on the choice of fibre references
\lambda(x)
and
\lambda(y)
.

If we have a choice of reference points in the fibre
\lambda:X\to\tilde{X}
, then we define a map
\mu_{\lambda}:\Pi{{X}}\to\Gamma
. We refer to this map as the
monodromy homomorphism
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
p:\tilde{X}\to X
be a
\Gamma
-covering. For a given pseudo-section of the covering
\lambda:X\to\tilde{X}
(i.e. a not necessarily continuous map satisfying
p\circ\lambda=\iden_{X}
), The
monodromy homomorphism
is a groupoid homomorphism
\nu_{\lambda}:\Pi{{X}}\to\Gamma
, where for
[\gamma]\in\Pi{{X}}(x,y)
, the group element
\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma])
is the unique element such that

\hslash_{\lambda(x)}([\gamma])\in\Pi{{\tilde{X}}}(\lambda(x),\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma])\cdot\lambda(y)).

(31)

Because of the uniqueness of lifts of homotopy classes of paths rel end points,
\mu_{\lambda}
is a well-defined map.
Lemma

3.4
shows that
\mu_{\lambda}
is indeed a groupoid homomorphism.

Lemma 3.4
.

The monodromy homomorphism
\mu_{\lambda}
of a
\Gamma
-covering is indeed a groupoid homomorphism. In other words, it sends homotopy classes of constant paths to the identity, and respects composition:

\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma_{0}\cdot\gamma_{1}])=\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma_{0}])\cdot\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma_{1}]).

(32)

Proof.

Since the homotopy class of the constant path
[\gamma=x]
lifts to
[\tilde{\gamma}=\lambda(x)]
,
\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma=x])=e
.

We now verify that
\mu_{\lambda}
respects composition. Let
\tilde{\gamma}_{i}
be the lifts of
\gamma_{i}
, and
\tilde{\gamma}
be the lift of
\gamma
. For convenience of notation, let us denote
\tilde{x}_{i}=\lambda(\gamma_{i}(0))
,
g_{i}=\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma_{i}])
for
i=0,1
, and
g=\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma])
. We align the end point
g_{0}\cdot\tilde{x}_{1}
of
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}
with the starting point
\tilde{x}_{1}
of
\tilde{\gamma_{1}}
by considering the shifted path
g_{0}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
. The concatenation
\hat{\gamma}=\tilde{\gamma}_{0}\cdot(g_{0}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}_{1})
gives us a lift of
\gamma
with base point
\tilde{x}_{0}
, and end point
\hat{\gamma}(1)=g_{0}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}_{1}(1)=(g_{0}\cdot g_{1})\cdot\tilde{x}_{2}
. By uniqueness of lifts, we have
\hat{\gamma}=\tilde{\gamma}
. In particular,
\tilde{\gamma}(1)=\hat{\gamma}(1)
implies
(g_{0}\cdot g_{1})\cdot\tilde{x}_{2}=g\cdot\tilde{x}_{2}
. Since the action is free, we thus conclude that
g=g_{0}\cdot g_{1}
.
∎

The following lemma describes the effect of the choice of pseudo-section on the the monodromy homomorphism.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
p:\tilde{X}\to X
be a
\Gamma
-covering,and consider two pseudo-sections
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}:X\to\tilde{X}
of the covering. Let
\theta:X\to\Gamma
be the assignment such that
\lambda^{\prime}(x)=\theta(x)^{-1}\cdot\lambda(x)
. Then for
[\gamma]\in\Pi{{X}}(x,y)

\mu_{\lambda^{\prime}}([\gamma])=\theta(x)^{-1}\cdot\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma])\cdot{\theta(y)}.

(33)

In other words,
\theta
defines a natural transformation
\theta:\mu_{\lambda}\Rightarrow\mu_{\lambda^{\prime}}
in the category of groupoids.

Proof.

For convenience of notation denote
x=\gamma(0)
,
y=\gamma(1)
,
\tilde{x}=\lambda(\gamma(0))
and
\tilde{y}=\lambda(\gamma(1))
. Let
\tilde{\gamma}:(I,0)\to(\tilde{X},\tilde{x})
be the
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}:(I,0)\to(\tilde{X},\theta_{x}\cdot\tilde{x})
lifts of
\gamma
to the two different base points. Due to the uniqueness of lifts of paths,
\theta_{x}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}=\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
. In particular,
\theta_{x}^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}(1)=\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(1)
. Substituting
eq.

31
,
\theta_{x}^{-1}\cdot\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma])\cdot\tilde{y}=\mu_{\lambda^{\prime}}([\gamma])\cdot\tilde{y}^{\prime}=\mu_{\lambda^{\prime}}([\gamma])\cdot\theta_{y}^{-1}\cdot\tilde{y}
. Because the action is free, we obtain
eq.

33
.
∎

Consider the restriction
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}:{\pi_{1}}({{X},{x}})\to\Gamma
of the monodromy homomorphism to the fundamental group. Since
\mu_{\lambda}
is a groupoid homomorphism (
Lemma

3.4
), the restriction to a vertex group of the domain
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
is a group homomorphism. In topological terms,
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
sends a class of loop
[\gamma]
based at
x\in X
to the unique deck transformation such that

\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}([\gamma])\cdot\hslash_{\lambda(x)}([\gamma])(1)=\lambda({x}).

(34)

We note that
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
only depends on the choice of reference point
\lambda(x)
of
x
in the fibre, and not any other point. If we choose a different choice of reference point in the fibre
\lambda^{\prime}(x)
,
Lemma

3.5
implies we induce an inner automorphism:

\mu_{\theta\cdot\lambda\rvert x}=\theta^{-1}\cdot\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}\cdot{\theta},

(35)

where
\theta\in\Gamma
is the unique element such that
\lambda(x)=\theta\cdot\lambda^{\prime}(x)
.

The monodromy group homomorphism
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
encodes topological information about the
\Gamma
-covering. We first consider a statement in terms of path-connected coverings.

Proposition 3.6

(
[
13
, Proposition 1.31,39-40]
)
.

Suppose
p:\tilde{X}\to X
is a path-connected
\Gamma
-covering. We have a short exact sequence
