\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(36)

Letting
T:\Gamma\times\tilde{X}\to\tilde{X}
denote the covering space action on
\tilde{X}
, the group of deck transformations
\mathrm{Aut}({p})
is isomorphic to
\Gamma
:

\mathrm{Aut}({p})=\left\{{T(g,\cdot):\tilde{X}\to\tilde{X}}\ \mid\ {g\in\Gamma}\right\}.

(37)

In fact, the monodromy homomorphism (up to isomorphism) classifies path-connected
\Gamma
-covering spaces.

Theorem 3.7

(
[
13
, Theorem 1.38-40]
)
.

There is a bijection between isomorphism classes of path-connected
\Gamma
-coverings, and normal subgroups of
{\pi_{1}}({X,x})
. Two
\Gamma
-coverings have the same monodromy homomorphism kernel iff they are isomorphic coverings; and every normal subgroup
H\triangleleft{\pi_{1}}({X,x})
has a path-connected
\Gamma
-covering with monodromy homomorphism kernel
H
.

If
\tilde{X}
is not path-connected,
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
is no longer surjective, though
{\pi_{1}}({p})
is still a monomorphism whose image is the kernel of
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
. The image of
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
in
\Gamma
encodes information about the connected components of
\tilde{X}
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Consider
p:\tilde{X}\twoheadrightarrow X
be a
\Gamma
-covering, where
X
is path-connected. Let
\{\tilde{X}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
be the set of connected components of
\tilde{X}
, and
p_{i}:\tilde{X}_{i}\to X
be the restriction of
p
to
\tilde{X}_{i}
. Let
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}:{\pi_{1}}({X,x})\to\Gamma
be a monodromy homomorphism, and
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the image of
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
. Then

•

All restrictions
p_{i}
are isomorphic
\Gamma^{\prime}
-coverings of
X
;

•

We have a bijection between left cosets of
\Gamma^{\prime}
in
\Gamma
and connected components of
\tilde{X}
.

Proof.

Consider a connected component
\tilde{X}_{i}
of
\tilde{X}
, and let
p_{i}
be the restriction of
p
to
\tilde{X}_{i}
. We first show that
p_{i}
is a surjection onto
X
. Consider some
x
in the image of
p_{i}
. Since
X
is path-connected, there is a path
\gamma
from
x
to any
y\in X
. Because
p
is a covering,
\gamma
lifts to a path
\tilde{\gamma}
based at
\tilde{x}_{i}\in p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)}\cap\tilde{X}_{i}
, satisfying
p\circ\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma
. Evaluating this at
t=1
, we thus have a point path connected to
\tilde{x}_{i}
that is in
p^{-1}{\left({y}\right)}
. Thus, for any
y\in X
, there is an element in
\tilde{X}_{i}
that projects onto
y
, i.e.
p_{i}
is a surjection onto
X
.

Recall
p
is the quotient of
\tilde{X}
by a
\Gamma
action that satisfies the covering space action criterion
(C2)
. The subgroup
\Gamma_{i}\subset\Gamma
that sends
\tilde{X}_{i}
to itself also satisfies
(C2)
, and thus
p_{i}
is a path-connected
\Gamma_{i}
-covering of
X
. Note that since
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
is obtained by querying lifts of paths,
\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
of
p
is the monodromy homomorphism of
p_{i}
if
\lambda(x)=\tilde{x}_{i}
. Applying
Proposition

3.6
, we have
\Gamma_{i}\cong\imag\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}=\Gamma^{\prime}
. Furthermore, the second half of
Proposition

3.6
implies the set of automorphisms of
p_{i}
is given by
\Gamma^{\prime}

\mathrm{Aut}({p_{i}})=\left\{{T(g,\cdot):\tilde{X}_{i}\to\tilde{X}_{i}}\ \mid\ {g\in\Gamma^{\prime}}\right\}.

Consider some other connected component
\tilde{X}_{j}
of
\tilde{X}
. Since the covering space action is free and transitive on fibres of
p
, if we pick some
x\in X
, and
\tilde{x}_{i}\in p^{-1}{\left({x}\right)}\cap\tilde{X}
, there a unique element
g_{ij}\in\Gamma
, such that
\tilde{x}_{j}=g_{ij}\cdot\tilde{x}_{i}
. Since the action of
g_{ij}
on
\tilde{X}
is a homeomorphism and must send each connected component to a homeomorphic connected components, the action of
g_{ij}
restricted to
\tilde{X}_{i}
is a homeomorphism sending
\tilde{X}_{i}
to
\tilde{X}_{j}
. Furthermore, since
p
is the quotient of the
\Gamma
action,
p_{j}=p_{i}\circ T(g_{ij},\cdot)
, where
T:\Gamma\times\tilde{X}\to\tilde{X}
denotes the
\Gamma
-action on
\tilde{X}
. Thus,
T(g_{ij},\cdot)
is a deck transformation between
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
. This implies
\tilde{X}
consists of isomorphic path-connected coverings over
X
.
\Gamma_{i}\cong\Gamma_{j}\cong\Gamma^{\prime}
.

The
\Gamma
-group action on
\tilde{X}
descends to a
\Gamma
-group action on its space of connected components
\pi_{0}\tilde{X}
. Because the action of
\Gamma
on
\tilde{X}
is a covering space action,
\Gamma
descends to a transitive action on
\pi_{0}\tilde{X}
; in particular, we have
\Gamma\cdot\tilde{X}_{i}=\pi_{0}\tilde{X}
. Thus the orbit stabiliser theorem implies there is a bijection between
\pi_{0}\tilde{X}
and cosets of the stabiliser of
\tilde{X}_{i}
. However, the stabiliser of
\tilde{X}_{i}
is precisely the group actions that send points on
\tilde{X}_{i}
to points on
\tilde{X}_{i}
, i.e.
\imag\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
. Thus, we have a bijection between
\pi_{0}\tilde{X}
and cosets of
\imag\mu_{\lambda\rvert x}
.
∎

3.2
Transition Maps of the Covering

We consider
\Gamma
-coverings that satisfy a more stringent topological condition than
(C1)
: that is, the base space
X
admits a good cover
\mathcal{U}
. Since each
U_{i}\in\mathcal{U}
is contractible, the homotopy lifting of
U_{i}
to
\tilde{X}
implies there is a chart
\varphi_{i}:p^{-1}{\left({U_{i}}\right)}\to U_{i}\times\Gamma
. We call the set of local trivialisations
\left\{{\varphi_{i}}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
the atlas corresponding to the covering
\mathcal{U}
.

Given the atlas
\left\{{\varphi_{i}}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
, the system of transition maps is the collection of maps
\psi_{ji}=\varphi_{j}\circ\varphi_{i}^{-1}:(U_{i}\cap U_{j})\times\Gamma\to(U_{i}\cap U_{j})\times\Gamma
where
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Because
\mathcal{U}
is a good cover,
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
is connected. Furthemore, since
\Gamma
is discrete, there is a unique
t_{ij}\in\Gamma
such that transition maps correspond to a local group action
\psi_{ji}(x,g)=(x,g\cdot t_{ij})
. Due to
\psi_{ki}=\psi_{kj}\psi_{ji}
, the equivariance of
\varphi_{i}
implies
t_{ij}t_{jk}=t_{ik}
for
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\cap U_{k}\neq\emptyset
. By
Lemma

2.8
,
t
extends to an edge groupoid homomorphism
t:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\to\Gamma
, whose evaluation on edges of the nerve yields the group elements
t_{ij}
that specify
\psi_{ji}
. We can
t
the
transition homomorphism
of the atlas.

Given a transition homomorphism associated to an atlas, we can reconstruct the covering up to isomorphism by gluing together local models of the covering space
U_{i}\times\Gamma
on overlaps. We recall the following facts about transition maps of principal
\Gamma
-bundles, specialised to
\Gamma
-coverings and good covers as in our setting; see for example
[
15
]
for a detailed exposition of on the subject.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
X
be a space with a good cover
\mathcal{U}=\left\{{U_{i}}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
.

•

For any groupoid homomorphism
t:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\to\Gamma
, there is a
\Gamma
-covering of
p:\tilde{X}\twoheadrightarrow X
and an atlas
\left\{{\varphi_{i}:p^{-1}{\left({U_{i}}\right)}\to U_{i}\times\Gamma}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
of the covering, such that its transition homomorphism is precisely
t

[
15
, §5 Theorem 3.2]
.

•

Consider two
\Gamma
-coverings
p:\tilde{X}\to X
and
p^{\prime}:\tilde{X}^{\prime}\to X
of
X
. There is a
\Gamma
-equivariant automorphism
\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}({p})
relating
p^{\prime}=p\circ\alpha
, iff for any arbitrary choices of atlases for
p
and
p^{\prime}
, the transition homomorphisms
t,t^{\prime}:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\to\Gamma
are related by a natural isomorphism
\theta:t\Rightarrow t^{\prime}

[
15
, §5 Prop 2.5]
.

We now relate the transition homomorphism with the monodromy homomorphism. Since the transition homomorphism is defined on the edge groupoid of the nerve of a good cover, while monodromy is defined on the fundamental groupoid of the underlying space, we relate the two homomorphisms via the realisation and snapping groupoid homomorphisms
R
and
S
. We show that by choosing compatible choices of pseudo-sections and atlases, monodromy – an attachment of
\Gamma
to any class of paths in
X
– is an extension of the data encoded by the transition homomorphism on the nerve of a good cover of the space.

Proposition 3.10
.

Consider
p:\tilde{X}\to X
a
\Gamma
-covering of a space with good cover
\mathcal{U}=\left\{{U_{i}}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
. Let
S:\Pi{{X}}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}:R
be the equivalence of categories as specified in
Proposition

2.12
, such that
SR(i)=i
for
i\in\mathcal{I}
. Then given a pseudo-section
\lambda:X\to\tilde{X}
:

•

There is a choice of atlas
\left\{{\varphi_{i}}\right\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
, such that the transition homomorphism
t:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\to\Gamma
is the pullback of the monodromy homomorphism along the realisation homomorphism:
t=\mu_{\lambda}\circ R
;

•

The following diagram commutes iff the pseudo-section is given by
\lambda(x)=\varphi_{S(x)}(x,e)
:

\hbox to144.92pt{\vbox to50.99pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 72.45946pt\lower-20.74995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{36.27905pt}{-5.75389pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(38)

Proof.

To prove the first point, we show that
for any pseudo-section
\lambda
of the covering, and any choice of transition homomorphism
t
, there is a unique natural isomorphism
\theta:\mu_{\lambda}\circ R\Rightarrow t
. Thus by
Proposition

3.9
there is a choice of atlas such that
\theta
is the identity and the transition homomorphism
t
is a sample of the monodromy
\mu_{\lambda}
. Since the covering group action is free and transitive on fibres of
p
, for any pseudo-section
\lambda
, we can find a unique assignment
\theta:\mathcal{I}\to\Gamma
, such that

\lambda(R(i))=\varphi^{-1}_{i}(R(i),\theta(i)).

Consider an edge
ij
in
\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}
and the realisation of edge path
R(ij)\in\Pi{{X}}(R(i),R(j))
. By definition there is a representative path
\gamma
of the class
R(ij)
that is carried by
ij
. In other words
\gamma\subset U_{i}\cup U_{j}
with
\gamma(0)\in U_{i}
and
\gamma(1)\in U_{j}
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is a good cover,
U_{i}\cup U_{j}
is simply connected, due to homotopy lifting properties, we can choose a section
s_{ij}:U_{i}\cup U_{j}\to\tilde{X}
such that
s_{ij}(R(i))=\lambda(R(i))=\varphi^{-1}_{i}(R(i),\theta_{i})
. Since this is a section of the covering,
s_{ij}
is uniquely specified by where it sends
R(i)
. In particular, since
\varphi_{i}^{-1}(\cdot,\theta_{i})
is a section, uniqueness of lifts implies
s_{ij}\rvert_{U_{i}}=\varphi_{i}^{-1}(\cdot,\theta_{i})
. Consider
Y_{ij}=s_{ij}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})
, and the map
\varphi_{j}\circ s_{ij}\rvert_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}:Y_{ij}\to U_{j}\times\Gamma
; since
s_{ij}\rvert_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}=\varphi_{i}^{-1}(\cdot,\theta_{i})\rvert_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}
, we have
\varphi_{j}\circ s_{ij}\rvert_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}(x)=\psi_{ji}(x,\theta_{i})=(x,\theta_{i}t_{ij})
. Because
\varphi_{j}
is a homeomorphism,
s_{ij}\rvert_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}=\varphi_{j}^{-1}(\cdot,\theta_{i}t_{ij})\rvert_{U_{i}\cap U_{j}}
. We now note that
\varphi_{j}^{-1}(\cdot,\theta_{i}t_{ij})
and
s_{ij}\rvert_{U_{j}}
are lifts of
U_{j}
to the covering space, and lifts are uniquely determined by where they send a single point. Since the two lifts coincide on the overlap
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
, we deduce that
s_{ij}\rvert_{U_{j}}=\varphi_{j}^{-1}(\cdot,\theta_{i}t_{ij})\rvert_{U_{j}}
.

Due to uniqueness of lifts, the lift of
\gamma
to
\tilde{X}
such that
\tilde{\gamma}(0)=\lambda(R(i))
is
\tilde{\gamma}=s_{ij}\circ\gamma
. Because
\gamma(1)=R(j)
, we have
\tilde{\gamma}(1)=\varphi_{j}^{-1}(R(j),\theta_{i}t_{ij})
. Because
\varphi_{j}^{-1}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant,
\tilde{\gamma}(1)=\theta_{i}t_{ij}\theta_{j}^{-1}\cdot\varphi_{j}^{-1}(R(j),\theta_{j})=\theta_{i}t_{ij}\theta_{j}^{-1}\cdot\lambda(R(j))
. Therefore, the definition of
\mu_{\lambda}
implies
\mu_{\lambda}\circ R([ij])=\theta_{i}t_{ij}\theta_{j}^{-1}
. Extending to all classes of edge paths by composition of edges, the assignment
\theta:\mathcal{I}\to\Gamma
specifies a natural isomorphism
\theta:\mu_{\lambda}\circ R\Rightarrow t
.

Recall we have a natural isomorphism
\varsigma_{\bullet}:\iden_{\Pi{{X}}}\Rightarrow RS
, where
\varsigma_{x}
is the unique class of paths in
\Pi{{X}}(x,RS(x))
with a representative path
s_{x}
contained in
U_{S(x)}
. Thus
\mu_{\lambda}\circ RS([\gamma])=\mu_{\lambda}(\varsigma_{x})\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma])\mu_{\lambda}^{-1}(\varsigma_{y})
. We choose a section
\sigma:U_{S(x)}\to p^{-1}{\left({U_{S(x)}}\right)}
, such that
\sigma(\cdot)=\varphi_{S(x)}(\cdot,\theta(S(x)))
. In particular, on the end points of
s_{x}
, we have
\sigma(x)=\varphi_{S(x)}(x,\theta(S(x)))
and
\sigma(RS(x))=\varphi_{S(x)}(RS(x),\theta(S(x)))
. The section lifts the path
s_{x}
to a path between
\sigma(x)
and
\sigma(RS(x))
.

For monodromy, we choose a pseudo-section
\lambda_{S}(\cdot)=\varphi_{S(\cdot)}(\cdot,\theta(S(\cdot)))
determined purely by
S
, the choise of
\theta:\mathcal{I}\to\Gamma
, and the atlas
\varphi_{\cdot}
. For the path
s_{x}
, the unique lifted path based at
\lambda_{S}(x)=\sigma(x)
has the end point

\sigma(RS(x))=\varphi_{S(x)}(RS(x),\theta(S(x)))=\varphi_{SRS(x)}(RS(x),\theta(SRS(x)))=\lambda_{S}(x),

because we have chosen
R,S
such that
SR(i)=i
for all indices of cover elements. Thus, if we choose
\lambda=\lambda_{S}
, then
\mu_{\lambda}([s_{x}])=\mu_{\lambda}(\varsigma_{x})=e
. Therefore we obtain
\mu_{\lambda}\circ RS=\mu_{\lambda}
. Conversely, if
\mu_{\lambda}=\mu_{\lambda}\circ RS
for all classes of paths, then for any
x\in U_{i}
such that
i=S(x)
, and
\varsigma_{x}^{-1}\in\Pi{{X}}(R(i),x)
, we have
\mu_{\lambda}(\varsigma_{x}^{-1})=e
. In other words, the choice of pseudo-section for the monodromy homomorphism is the lift along end paths of points
\lambda(x)=\lambda_{S}(x)
. Repeat for all points
x\in S^{-1}{\left({i}\right)}
, and all
i\in\mathcal{I}
, we thus obtain
\mu_{\lambda}=\mu_{\lambda}\circ RS
implying
\lambda=\lambda_{S}
.

If we set
\theta=e
, then we have
t=\mu_{\lambda}\circ R
. Choosing
\lambda=\lambda_{S}
, we have
\mu_{\lambda}=\mu_{\lambda}\circ RS
, thus implying
\mu_{\lambda}=t\circ S
.
∎

3.3
Induced Coverings

Given a
\Gamma
-covering
p:\tilde{Y}\twoheadrightarrow Y
, a map
f:X\to Y
from a space
X
satisfying the topological criterion
(C1)
, consider the pullback of the pair of maps
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xleftarrow{p}\tilde{Y}
:

\hbox to81.97pt{\vbox to49.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.81195pt\lower-23.34724pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.82123pt}{-13.47365pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(39)

Explicitly, the pullback space
\tilde{X}
is given by

\tilde{X}=X\times_{Y}\tilde{Y}=\left\{{(x,\tilde{y})\in X\times\tilde{Y}}\ \mid\ {f(x)=p(\tilde{y})}\right\},

(40)

and
f^{\ast}p
is the restriction of the projection
X\times\tilde{Y}\to X
on
\tilde{X}
. Similarly, the map
f_{\sharp}
is the restriction of the projection
X\times\tilde{Y}\to\tilde{Y}
on
\tilde{X}
. We recall that
p:\tilde{Y}\twoheadrightarrow Y
is a
\Gamma
-covering, and
f:X\to Y
is a map between spaces satisfying
(C1)
, then:

1.

The map
f^{\ast}p:\tilde{X}\to X
in the pullback
eq.

39
is a
\Gamma
-covering;

2.

The map
f_{\sharp}
in the pullback
eq.

39
is equivariant w.r.t. the
\Gamma
-covering space actions on
\tilde{X}
and
\tilde{Y}
respectively.

Example 3.11
.

Let
f:I\to S^{1}
be the map that identifies the end points of the interval to form a circle. Let
p:\R\to S^{1}
be the universal covering
p(t)=e^{2\pi it}
of
S^{1}
. Then the induced covering space is given by
\tilde{X}=\left\{{(t,t+n)}\ \mid\ {t\in[0,1],\ n\in\Z}\right\}
, the covering map
f^{\ast}p
and
f_{\sharp}
are the projections onto the first and second factors respectively. Note that the projection map
f_{\sharp}
is a surjection onto
\R
as it glues together end points of disjoint intervals in
\tilde{X}
.

Let us consider then the monodromy homomorphism of the induced covering. We show that if the pseudo-section
\lambda:X\to\tilde{X}
is chosen carefully, the monodromy homomorphism
\mu^{\ast}_{\lambda}:\Pi{{X}}\to\Gamma
of the induced covering
f^{\ast}p
is the pullback of a monodromy homomorphism
\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}:\Pi{{Y}}\to\Gamma
of
p
along
f
. The choices
\lambda
and
\lambda_{Y}
need to be compatible; in other words, we choose
\lambda(x)=(x,\lambda_{Y}\circ f(x))
. This is equivalent to forcing
\lambda
to satisfy
f(a)=f(b)\iff f_{\sharp}\lambda(a)=f_{\sharp}\lambda(b)
. If this is not satisfied, then there are cases, such as
3.11
, where
\mu_{\lambda}
cannot be expressed as a pullback of
\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
f^{\ast}p:\tilde{X}\to X
be the pullback of a
\Gamma
-covering
p:\tilde{Y}\to Y
along a continuous map
f:X\to Y
, where
X
is path-connected. Suppose for some point
x\in X
, the pseudo-sections
\lambda_{Y}:Y\to\tilde{Y}
and
\lambda:X\to\tilde{X}
satisfy
f_{\sharp}\circ\lambda(x)=\lambda_{Y}\circ f(x)
. Then the pesudo-sections are compatible for all points in
X
iff
\mu_{\lambda}
is the pullback of
\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}
:

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}\circ\Pi{{f}}\iff f_{\sharp}\circ\lambda=\lambda_{Y}\circ f.

(41)

Proof.

We first proof a slightly weaker result first. If the lifts satisfy
f_{\sharp}\circ\lambda(x_{0})=\lambda_{Y}\circ f(x_{0})
, then for classes of paths between a pair of points
x_{0},x_{1}\in X
, we have

\displaystyle\forall[\gamma]\in\Pi{{X}}(x_{0},x_{1}),\quad\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma])=\mu^{\ast}_{\lambda_{Y}}\circ\Pi{{f}}([\gamma])\iff f_{\sharp}\circ\lambda(x_{1})=\lambda_{Y}\circ f(x_{1}).

(42)

We show this by considering path lifts in the induced covering. Let us consider a path
\gamma:I\to X
, from
\gamma(0)=x_{0}
to
\gamma(1)=x_{1}
, and
f\circ\gamma:I\to Y
. The former path admits a lift
\gamma^{\ast}
to
\tilde{X}
and the latter a lift
(f\circ\gamma)^{\ast}
to
\tilde{Y}
. If we choose base points of the lifts consistently, such that
(f\circ\gamma)^{\ast}(0)=f_{\sharp}\gamma^{\ast}(0)=\lambda_{Y}\circ f(x_{0})
, then by uniqueness of lifts, we have
(f\circ\gamma)^{\ast}=f_{\sharp}\circ\gamma^{\ast}
. This statement is expressed in the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(I,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\tilde{X},\tilde{x})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\tilde{Y},f_{\sharp}(\tilde{x}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(Y,f(x))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\circ\gamma)^{\ast}}

(43)

Consider then the monodromy homomorphisms
\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}:\Pi{{Y}}\to\Gamma
, and
\mu^{\ast}_{\lambda}:\Pi{{X}}\to\Gamma
defined by respective pseudo-sections
\lambda_{Y}
and
\lambda
. For brevity of notation, let
\tilde{y}_{i}=\lambda_{Y}\circ f(x_{i})
, and
\tilde{x}_{i}=\lambda(x_{i})
. From the definition of the monodromy homomorphism, we must have

\displaystyle f_{\sharp}\circ\gamma^{\ast}(1)

\displaystyle={f_{\sharp}}\mathopen{}\left({\mu^{\ast}_{\lambda}([\gamma])\cdot\tilde{x}_{1}}\right)\mathclose{}=\mu^{\ast}_{\lambda}([\gamma])\cdot f_{\sharp}(\tilde{x}_{1})

\displaystyle(f\circ\gamma)^{\ast}(1)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\lambda}([f\circ\gamma])\cdot\tilde{y}_{1},

where in the first line we use the fact that
f_{\sharp}
is an equivariant map. Because the upper triangle commutes in
eq.

43
,
(f\circ\gamma)^{\ast}=f_{\sharp}\circ\gamma^{\ast}
implies for any
[\gamma]\in\Pi{{X}}(x_{0},x_{1})
,

\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}([f\circ\gamma])\cdot\tilde{y}_{1}=\mu^{\ast}_{\lambda}([\gamma])\cdot f_{\sharp}(\tilde{x}_{1}).

(44)

Since the covering
p
satisfies
(C2)
, the action of
\Gamma
on
\tilde{y}
is free.

If we have
\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}([f\circ\gamma])=\mu^{\ast}_{\lambda}([\gamma])
for some
[\gamma]
, then
eq.

44
implies
\lambda_{Y}\circ f(x_{1})=f_{\sharp}\circ\lambda(x_{1})
due to the free group action. Conversely. If we have
f_{\sharp}\circ\lambda(x_{1})=\lambda_{Y}\circ f(x_{1})
, then due to the free group action, this implies
\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}([f\circ\gamma])=\mu^{\ast}_{\lambda}([\gamma])
for any
[\gamma]\in\Pi{{X}}(x_{0},x_{1})
.

We have thus proved
eq.

42
.
If
f_{\sharp}\circ\lambda=\lambda_{Y}\circ f
for all points, then
\mu^{\ast}_{\lambda}=\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}\circ\Pi{{f}}
for all homotopy classes of paths. Conversely, if for all homotopy classes of paths
\mu_{\lambda}=\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}\circ\Pi{{f}}
, and the lifts are synchronised
f_{\sharp}\circ\lambda(x)=\lambda_{Y}\circ f(x)
at some point
x
, then since
X
is path-connected, for any point
x^{\prime}
we can consider a path from
x
to
x^{\prime}
; then the fact that for
[\gamma]\in\Pi{{X}}(x,x^{\prime})
we have
\mu_{\lambda}([\gamma])=\mu_{\lambda_{Y}}\circ\Pi{{f}}([\gamma])
implies
f_{\sharp}\circ\lambda(x^{\prime})=\lambda_{Y}\circ f(x^{\prime})
.
∎

Since the pullback of a monodromy homomorphism is a monodromy homomorphism for some choice of lift, we can choose a compatible atlas whose transition homomorphism is a sample of the pulled back monodromy homomorphism.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
f^{\ast}p:\tilde{X}\to X
be the pullback of a
\Gamma
-covering
p:\tilde{Y}\to Y
along a continuous map

(45)

Proof.

We choose the lift of points in
X
to the induced covering space
\tilde{X}
satisfying the conditions of
Lemma

3.12
. Since
\mu_{\lambda}\circ\Pi{{f}}
is equal to the monodromy homomorphism of such a lift, then we can choose the atlas as specified in
Proposition

3.10
such that its transition homomorphism is a sample of the monodromy.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

While we can sample from a pullback monodromy to recover a transition homomorphism, in general we cannot extend a transition homomorphism of a pullback covering to recover the pullback monodromy. That is because the extension requires a type of lift of points in the induced covering specified in
Proposition

3.10
, that might not be compatible with the requirements on the lift as specified in
Lemma

3.12
.

Remark 3.15
.

Consider a simplicial map
L\xrightarrow{\psi}K
. If we have a map
f:|K|\to X
, because the realisation functor
R
is a natural transformation
R:\mathcal{E}\Rightarrow\Pi
, the combination of
Proposition

3.13
and
Proposition

2.14
implies if
f^{\ast}t:\mathcal{E}{{K}}\to\Gamma
and
(f\circ|\Psi|)^{\ast}t:\mathcal{E}{{L}}\to\Gamma
are transition homomorphism derived from the same monodromy homomorphism
\mu_{\lambda}
, then we can write
(f\circ|\Psi|)^{\ast}t=f^{\ast}t\circ\mathcal{E}{{\Psi}}
.

Equivalence up to natural isomorphism allows us to make some statements about the fundamental groups of
X
and
Y
. In particular, if we choose the covering
p
on
Y
to be the universal covering of
Y
, then the transition homomorphism of the pullback covering recovers the homomorphism on the fundamental group induced by the map
f:X\to Y
up to isomorphism.

By restricting
Proposition

3.13
from groupoids to vertex groups of the groupoids, we immediately obtain the following.

.

Let
f^{\ast}p:\tilde{X}\to X
be the pullback of the universal covering
p:\tilde{Y}\to Y
of

along a continuous map

. Assume
X
admits a good cover
\mathcal{U}
, and let

f^{\ast}p
. Then the group homomorphism

recovers the induced homomorphism of
f
on the fundamental group up to isomorphism:

(46)

Corollary

3.16
immediately suggests that
{\pi_{1}}({f})
is encoded on finite data
t^{\ast}
specified on the edges of the nerve, one that can be obtained by realising those edges as explicit paths, and lifting the images of the paths in the universal cover of
X
. We emphasise that
t^{\ast}
is dependent on a choice of atlas in the pullback covering
f^{\ast}p
, as stated in
Proposition

3.13
. As a change of atlas – that is, a gauge transformation – induces a natural transformation between the groupoid homomorphisms (
Proposition

3.9
), the induced homomorphisms on the edge group is uniquely defined up to conjugation. Thus, if we have different spaces mapped into
Y
, we cannot meaningfully compare the images of their fundamental/edge groups in
\Gamma
due to dependency on such choices. However, we can mod out the effect of the gauge symmetry by comparing the conjugacy classes of the image of loops. Since
\Gamma
is isomorphic to the fundamental group of
Y
, the set of conjugacy classes of
\Gamma
are in bijective correspondence with
[S^{1},Y]
, the homotopy types of maps from
S^{1}
into
Y
.

An even simpler approach to mod out the conjugacy would be to work with homology groups instead. If we apply the groupoid homology functor
\mathcal{H}_{1}:\Grpd\to\AbGrp
to
eq.

45
in
Proposition

3.13
, the equivalence between the first groupoid homology of the fundamental group and first singular homology, as well as that between the first groupoid homology of the edge groupoid of the nerve and first simplicial homology, yields a homomorphism
\tau:{H_{1}}\mathopen{}\left({\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}\right)\mathclose{}\to{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{ab}}
that recovers the induced homomorphism of
f
on singular homology groups.

Proposition 3.17
.

Consider a space
X
admitting a good cover
\mathcal{U}
. Let
f:X\to Y
be a continuous map, and
f^{\ast}p:\tilde{X}\to X
be the covering map induced from the universal covering of
Y
. Let
t:\mathcal{E}{{\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{}}}\to\Gamma
be a transition homomorphism of the covering
f^{\ast}p
. Then for any abelian group
A
, we have a well-defined group homomorphism
\tau:H_{1}(\mathsf{N}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathclose{};A)\to{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{ab}}\otimes A
given by,

\tau:\left[\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\otimes a_{i}\right]\mapsto\sum_{i}{t}^{\mathsf{ab}}([\sigma_{i}])\otimes a_{i},

(47)

which recovers the induced homomorphism of
f
on homology up to isomorphism

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.85542pt}{-15.40889pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
